1. Document Control, Source Hierarchy and Engineering Maturity

This FDP is the complete consolidated successor to FDP v8. It integrates
the original FDP, the FEED Class 3 initial draft, the
PDVSA–PetroUrdaneta planning workbook, the binary production forecast,
the La Paz well-reactivation workbook, the EOR water-injection
statement, the La Paz Sur technical memorandum, the 2016 gas
chromatography report and the approved owner changes. The source files
remain controlled records; this FDP provides the integrated development
interpretation.

The document is not a final process design. Line sizes, relief loads,
ESD/PSD setpoints, valve closure times, equipment duties, generator
ratings, flare radiation zones, flexible-pipe specifications and
injection limits remain subject to controlled FEED calculations. API,
NORSOK, COVENIN and applicable Venezuelan requirements shall be mapped
in the design-basis and regulatory registers before package procurement.

2. Executive Summary

PetroUrdaneta’s development strategy is a phased, modular restoration
and expansion of the La Paz-area production system, followed by Mara,
Mara West, La Paz Sur, El Moján and later infill opportunities. The
development prioritizes reactivation before infill, early use of
existing infrastructure, self-contained EPFs and incremental
central-facility upgrades. The production system is deliberately based
on normal multiphase flow from the well to the EPF and from the EPF to
the integrated FSB/CPF complex.

The owner-approved reference concept contains 38 Phase 1 wells,
comprising 22 PCP/ePCP wells, 5 ESP wells, 8 retained gas-lift wells and
3 wells whose lift method remains to be selected. Twelve distributed
EPFs gather several wells each. Every EPF contains a local multiphase
manifold, chemical-injection provisions, one VFD-controlled multiphase
booster package, bypass, non-return and emergency-isolation valves, two
gas gensets, switchgear, SCADA and a pig-valve launcher. Larger piggable
trunklines connect the EPFs to one central manifold at the integrated
FSB/CPF complex.

At FSB, pig receivers feed a permanent modular two-phase slug-handling
vessel with isolation and full-flow bypass before the main three-phase
separator. The separator divides production into gas, crude and produced
water. Gas is compressed centrally and allocated to field/FSB fuel,
eight gas-lift wells, early Campo Boscan export and later NGL recovery
and Palmarejo LNG. Crude passes through EFB storage, transfer pumps and
a LACT unit, then to the two 80,000-bbl Palmarejo tanks and the
lake-export terminal. Produced water is treated, stored and reinjected
for disposal and pressure support/EOR.

The original FDP v8 reported approximately 8.6 billion barrels OOIP, 4.6
Tcf OGIP, a current La Paz baseline near 1,300 BOPD, a 13,500-BOPD Phase
1 plateau, and a 40,000-BOPD strategic full-field target [S01]. The
PDVSA/business-planning and binary forecast sources contain larger
portfolio cases, including approximately 27–28 kbopd oil, 49–52 MMscfd
gas and long-term produced-water rates approaching 96.9 kbwpd [S03]
[S04]. These are different scopes and are maintained as separate design
envelopes.

The present configuration is credible as a modular development concept
but is not yet a frozen facilities design. The highest-priority closures
are the controlled well-by-well production and pressure basis; FSB
brownfield integrity/capacity; integrated multiphase transient model;
multiphase-pump operating envelope; pigging and slug-vessel sizing; gas
allocation and composition; water-treatment/injectivity basis;
power-system stability and black start; relief/flare/depressuring study;
and terminal/marine assurance.

[]

Integrated field-to-market architecture

3. Asset, Concession and Existing Infrastructure

The PetroUrdaneta portfolio covers La Paz, Mara, Mara West, La Paz Sur
and El Moján. La Paz is the Phase 1 cash-flow engine because it has
existing producing wells, shut-in reactivation candidates, the FSB
gathering/processing hub, the LF-B gas-compression system and access to
Palmarejo export infrastructure. Development beyond La Paz must remain
gated by production performance, subsurface confidence, infrastructure
availability and partner/concession approvals.

Existing FSB capacity is reported at approximately 5,000 BOPD and 0.9
MMscfd, while the LF-B gas-compression plant is reported at 19.5 MMscfd
[S01] [S10]. These values are not design guarantees. The existing plant
requires a condition, capacity, relief, control-system and integrity
assessment before it can be credited for Phase 1. The main line to
Palmarejo has reported active leaks and has been treated as limited to
approximately 10,000 BOPD pending repairs. The subsurface line toward
Puerto Miranda lacks a current integrity and pigging basis.

The PDVSA–PetroUrdaneta workbook identifies upgrades to stations H7, L7,
K8, C, MRV1, MRV2 and MRV3 as multiphase pumping stations, a 20-slot
production/measurement manifold at EFB in two phases, a
water-treatment/injection plant from 2029, replacement of the PetroWayuu
Coriolis unit with a LACT unit, maritime cabotage as a contingency and
security/community programs. These items are part of the consolidated
infrastructure plan and require scope/ownership confirmation.

4. Subsurface, Reservoirs and Reserves

The development includes Eocene, Paleocene, Cretaceous and basement
intervals with materially different pressures, fluids, lift needs and
recovery mechanisms. The original FDP reports La Paz Eocene/Paleocene
pressure near 900 psi and Cretaceous pressure near 4,405 psi, while La
Paz Sur data report a Cretaceous initial pressure of approximately 4,340
psig at 9,000 ft and a 2025 estimate near 4,490 psi. These values
require depth-datum and fluid-system reconciliation.

The La Paz Sur memorandum reports 402 MMbbl STOIIP, a 26.1% recovery
factor, 105.05 MMbbl oil plus 32.57 Bscf gas, totaling 110.48 MMBOE, and
a minimum PDVSA commitment of 55.24 MMBOE [S07]. The reported
development area is 16.32 km², with average net pay 82.49 m, porosity
6.81%, water saturation 23.07%, formation volume factor 1.19, GOR 310
scf/STB and oil gravity 25° API. The proposed program contains fourteen
wells: two reactivations, two delineation/exploration wells and ten
development wells.

A material structural uncertainty exists. A prior interpretation
separated P-191 and P-201 through inferred faults, whereas the recent
technical view challenges the existence or sealing nature of those
faults. No Cretaceous oil-water contact has been established in the
evaluated block. The FDP therefore treats the 26.1% recovery factor and
development continuity as contingent on static/dynamic model
reconciliation and delineation evidence.

Water-injection evidence indicates reservoir-pressure recovery from a
trough near 1,200–1,400 psia toward approximately 1,700 psia at
cumulative production above 210 MMSTB [S06]. The 2026 year-to-date
injection average is reported near 1.2 MBWIPD versus 1.3 MBWIPD planned,
with a plan of 1.4 MBWIPD from July onward. The favorable pressure
response supports continued injection, but it does not replace
well-integrity, compatibility, fracture-margin, surveillance and
voidage-replacement studies.

Portfolio oil, gas and water facility envelope

The original schedule contains conflicting first-oil dates. Some
sections target first oil at Month 5, while other execution tables
indicate D+1. The consolidated basis treats the schedule as unapproved
until a single D0 definition, well sequence, package delivery plan and
facility-readiness gate are issued. Rigs must be coordinated with EPF,
line and FSB readiness. Following pump installation, each well undergoes
a 72-hour test; the rig may be released 12 hours after stable surface
flow is established, provided the well is secure and the test package is
independent of the rig.

6. Wells, Reactivation, Drilling and Constraints

Reactivation has priority over infill drilling. The FDP v8 describes 32
La Paz wells, the reactivation workbook lists 35 wells, the owner
concept uses 38 Phase 1 wells, and the binary model contains 86 unique
La Paz well names. These populations must be reconciled through a
controlled well master identifying ownership, status, completion,
reservoir, rate, pressure, lift, intervention, EPF and forecast case.

The reactivation workbook reports five wells with community/access
issues—P-108, P-173, P-95, P-180 and P-152—four wells with uneconomic
surface equipment—P-192, P-82, P-61 and P-88—P-161 requiring major
subsurface repair, P-91 with high water production and P-52 awaiting
abandonment [S05]. These constraints are development gates, not
footnotes. Each reactivation AFE must include rig days/cost,
completion/lift hardware, flowline/power tie-in, 72-hour test, waste
handling, expected production, uncertainty and abandonment fallback.

La Paz Sur requires a separate appraisal-to-development sequence. The
two reactivations and two delineation/exploration wells should precede
the final commitment to all ten development wells unless
partner-approved evidence justifies parallelization. The
structural-continuity uncertainty and absence of a defined OWC require
explicit subsurface decision gates.

7. Artificial Lift, Well Systems and Electrification

The confirmed Phase 1 concept contains 22 PCP/ePCP wells, 5 ESP wells
and 8 gas-lift wells, with three lift selections outstanding. ePCP
denotes a progressive cavity pump driven by a downhole electric motor
with no rotating shaft to surface. The ePCP protection philosophy
therefore resembles an ESP electrical completion while retaining
PCP-specific torque, viscosity, solids and differential-pressure
constraints.

ESP and ePCP wells are powered from the EPF through a high-voltage field
feeder, a wellsite step-down transformer, protection/MCC and a VFD.
Final voltages are intentionally not frozen. The electrical study must
include load flow, fault levels, motor starting, harmonic distortion,
cable ampacity/voltage drop, earthing, lightning, hazardous-area
interfaces, protection coordination and black start.

[]

ESP and ePCP well systems

The eight gas-lift wells remain on gas lift. Injection gas is compressed
at FSB, metered and controlled at the well, travels down the annulus and
enters the production tubing through gas-lift valves. Produced gas
returning with the wellstream is recovered at FSB; gas lift is therefore
a recycle loop, and only unrecovered losses should be treated as net
consumption.

8. Well Testing, Measurement and Production Allocation

The confirmed base case is a temporary modular well-test package at the
selected wellsite. Production is diverted through a controlled test
manifold to a three-phase test separator. Test gas is metered and flared
during the 72-hour test, while measured oil and water are recombined and
returned to the normal multiphase line toward the EPF. The EPFs do not
contain permanent test separators.

The test package requires a flare knockout vessel, radiation/dispersion
assessment, exclusion zone, ignition and flame monitoring, gas
detection, ESD, liquid carryover protection, stable flow measurement and
Ministry/environmental authorization. Routine atmospheric venting is not
accepted as a substitute for a test flare.

A Roxar-type multiphase flowmeter is retained as a lower-flare
alternative, subject to Ministry approval and project validation across
gas-volume fraction, viscosity, emulsion, water cut, flow regime and
PVT. The alternative keeps the full multiphase stream in the production
line, but it must demonstrate allocation accuracy against a separator
test.

[]

Mobile well-test package

9. Field Gathering and Standard Modular EPFs

Each EPF is a staffed, modular gathering, power and boosting facility.
It is not a local processing plant. Several wells flow through smaller
local multiphase laterals to a production manifold. The commingled
stream passes through a VFD-controlled multiphase booster, non-return
valve, ESD isolation and pig-valve launcher into the larger EPF-to-FSB
trunk. A full-flow bypass is provided for start-up, maintenance or
natural-flow operation only when validated by hydraulics.

The FEED draft identifies the following tag basis: MAN-101 production
manifold; TH-101 test header; CI-101 chemical injection; PMP-101
twin-screw multiphase pump; NRV-101 check valve; PL-101 pig launcher;
ESDV-101/102 emergency isolation; FG-101 fuel-gas package; GEN-101 gas
genset; and SCADA-101 controls. The final pump technology is not frozen.
Twin-screw, helico-axial or other qualified multiphase technologies
shall be compared against gas-volume fraction, viscosity, sand, suction
pressure, differential pressure, turndown, slug tolerance, seal
requirements, power and maintenance.

The owner basis is one modular multiphase booster package at every EPF.
Because this creates a local single-point dependency, FEED shall compare
duty-only plus mobile spare, installed duty/standby and rapid-change
cartridge/package strategies. Each package requires suction/discharge
isolation, check valve, pressure/temperature instrumentation,
minimum-flow or recycle philosophy where applicable, vibration/seal
monitoring, ESD integration and vendor-confirmed operating envelope.

Each EPF has two gas-engine generators. The gensets supply EPF loads and
the well-field electrical network through switchgear and a step-up
transformer. Fuel gas comes from the central gas system or an approved
local interface and requires scrubber/separation, pressure regulation,
metering, trip isolation and off-spec protection. The staffed EPF basis
includes a control-room operator, one or two field operators and
security personnel; occupied-building siting and evacuation must reflect
credible gas and fire scenarios.

A small closed-drain drum may be installed for defined intermittent
drains from the manifold, booster skid and pig launcher. It is not an
upset separator and shall not receive continuous production or full
pigging slugs. It requires independent high-level alarm, pressure/vacuum
protection, controlled vapor disposition and a closed, grounded
vacuum-truck connection.

[]

Standard modular EPF

10. Piggable Multiphase Trunk Network and Flexible Pipe

The field has two gathering levels. Well-to-EPF laterals are smaller
multiphase lines and may use qualified spoolable RTP/composite pipe
where pressure, temperature, chemistry, sour service, rapid-gas
decompression, gas permeation, sand, external damage and installation
criteria are satisfied. The larger EPF-to-FSB trunklines are the
piggable segment. Each trunk has a pig-valve launcher at its EPF and a
matching pig-valve receiver at the FSB central manifold.

All twelve trunklines converge on one central manifold. The architecture
is retained because it preserves independent EPF isolation and central
processing, but it creates congestion and a common receiving interface.
The central manifold shall allow individual trunk isolation, pressure
monitoring, pig detection, controlled routing to the slug vessel,
drain/vent isolation and maintenance without shutting all production
where practical.

The owner considers six-inch piggable flexible pipe a base screening
option. It is not frozen. Liquid-only screening shows that 4-, 6- and
8-inch clear bores yield materially different velocities, but
liquid-only velocity cannot size a multiphase line. FEED shall model
well/EPF minimum, normal, maximum and decline cases; gas fraction; water
cut; viscosity; temperature; route elevations; pump curves; FSB arrival
pressure; erosion; liquid holdup; slugging; shutdown and restart; and
pigging transients. Four-, six- and eight-inch internal-bore options
shall be compared per EPF or trunk class rather than forcing a uniform
diameter.

[]

Liquid-only 4/6/8-inch screening

Flexible-pipe piggability must be demonstrated for the complete
installed system. Qualification includes constant internal diameter,
bend radius, couplings/end fittings, full-bore pig valves, tees, pig
type, cleaning/inspection capability, pressure cycles, permeation,
rapid-gas decompression and mechanical protection. Steel remains the
fallback at Christmas trees, manifolds, pump nozzles, pig
launchers/receivers, ESD stations, vessels, compressors,
flare/depressuring, NGL and cryogenic LNG service.

[]

Twelve piggable trunks

11. Integrated FSB/CPF Inlet, Slug Handling and Main Separation

FSB and CPF are one integrated central complex. All twelve piggable
trunks terminate at pig receivers and the central manifold. A
permanently installed modular two-phase slug-handling vessel is located
upstream of the main three-phase separator. The vessel is required
because pigging and transient terrain/hydrodynamic behavior can deliver
concentrated liquid volumes that upset the main separator.

The slug vessel is not sized by judgment. Its working/surge volume must
come from integrated transient modeling of each trunk and coincident
operating cases. It requires inlet isolation, full-capacity bypass, gas
equalization, high-high level/pressure protection, controlled liquid
transfer, drains/vents and ESD. The bypass permits maintenance or a
validated operating route; it is not a depressuring system and shall not
be used to defeat protection.

The main separator receives a multiphase inlet. Gas bubbles rise into
the gas-disengagement zone, with a mist extractor limiting liquid
carryover. Oil settles above the water interface, while produced water
occupies the lower layer. Solids/sand collect in the bottom drainage
zone. Separate level/interface controls route gas, crude and water to
their respective treatment systems.

[]

FSB slug handling and main separator

The existing FSB cannot be credited for the Phase 1 or portfolio cases
until the brownfield assessment verifies vessel dimensions, internals,
control valves, relief capacity, corrosion allowance, foundations,
piping, supports, electrical classification, fire/gas coverage,
instrumentation and tie-in windows. Staged upgrades shall be based on an
approved production case rather than one fixed full-field capacity.

12. Gas Compression, Fuel Gas, Gas Lift and Central Flare

All gas compression is located at FSB. The process begins with liquid
protection at the compressor suction. Parallel or staged compressor
packages discharge through cooling and knockout to a central gas header.
The header feeds the eight gas-lift wells, EPF/FSB fuel gas,
treatment/Campo Boscan export and later Palmarejo LNG.

Centralization reduces distributed equipment but creates a common-mode
risk. The design requires train availability analysis, liquid carryover
protection, anti-surge control, trip sequencing, one-train-down
capacity, turndown/recycle, independent starting fuel, black-start
strategy, critical spares and load shedding. Compressor trips must not
create excessive upstream pressure; the cause-and-effect logic shall
sequence booster trips, well shut-in and trunk isolation while
preserving separator control.

[]

Central gas compression and flare

The central FSB flare is the primary relief and depressuring system for
the processing complex. It shall include a complete relief-case
register, segmented depressuring philosophy, flare-network hydraulics,
backpressure verification, knockout drum, liquid handling,
radiation/noise/dispersion assessment, ignition reliability, smokeless
capability if required and emergency power. A permanent flare at every
EPF is not the default. EPF pressure is normally controlled through safe
shutdown and isolation; maintenance inventory is displaced or recovered
where practical and otherwise managed through an approved controlled
vent or portable flare after dispersion and permit review.

13. Gas Quality, Treatment, NGL and Campo Boscan Export

The 2016 gas chromatography provides three screening compositions [S08].
The La Paz rich sample reports approximately 77.75 mol% methane, 11.90
mol% ethane, 6.21 mol% propane, 0.50 mol% CO₂, 15 ppm H₂S, 1,294 BTU/scf
and 3.342 GPM C3+. A flow-station sample reports 9.75 mol% CO₂,
approximately 190 ppm H₂S, 1,060 BTU/scf and 1.499 GPM C3+. A Mara
sample reports 2.35 mol% CO₂, approximately 8 ppm H₂S, 1,204 BTU/scf and
2.344 GPM C3+.

The variability is too large for one treatment design without fresh
sampling. The project shall obtain pressure-preserved samples by
reservoir/cluster and a blended FSB stream, including full composition,
water, H₂S, CO₂, nitrogen, mercury screening, hydrocarbon dew point and
representative pressure/temperature. Materials, corrosion, fuel-gas
derating, Boscan specification and LNG pretreatment depend on these
data.

The staged gas-treatment concept is modular. The early system uses inlet
separation/coalescing, acid-gas removal if required, pipeline
dehydration and hydrocarbon-dew-point control. TEG is a conventional
pipeline/fuel-gas dehydration option, while membranes, JT/MEG or
mechanical refrigeration may be advantageous depending on CO₂, pressure
and NGL economics. Condensed C3+ falls into an NGL receiver, then passes
to pressurized storage, metering and a dedicated road-loading bay.

[]

Gas treatment, NGL and Boscan export

[]

Screening gas-disposition envelope

[]

Fuel-gas sensitivity versus continuous power

A 5–6-MMscfd Campo Boscan pilot train is the preferred early export
concept, expandable through parallel modules toward 15 and 30 MMscfd
when surplus gas is firm. Natgas Global Resources has published a
6-MMscfd skid-mounted JT/MEG project and broader
compression/refrigeration integration experience; it is a credible
integrator candidate, not a selected vendor [E01] [E02]. The published
package pressure reduction and product basis shall not be assumed
suitable until Boscan provides receipt pressure, water/HC dew point,
CO₂, H₂S, heating value, contaminants and custody-metering requirements.

Gas accounting shall meter separator gas, gas-lift injection and return,
EPF/FSB fuel, flare/blowdown, Boscan export, NGL and later LNG. Gas lift
is a recycle; only leakage, flare and other unrecovered losses are
deducted as net consumption. Off-spec gas requires automated isolation
and a defined recycle, curtailment or controlled flare route.

14. Palmarejo LNG and ISO-Tank Export

Palmarejo LNG is a deferred CAPEX stage implemented after the gas
balance, Boscan strategy, fresh composition and market/logistics are
established. All field and FSB compression remains at FSB. Dry treated
gas is sent to the liquefaction facility close to the Palmarejo
terminal, where final pressure conditioning and cryogenic pretreatment
are provided.

TEG alone is not an LNG pretreatment basis. The Palmarejo train may
require acid-gas polishing, mercury removal, molecular-sieve
dehydration, heavy-hydrocarbon/freeze-out control and nitrogen
confirmation. Galileo Cryobox modules are scalable, but the module count
and production are subject to a site-specific vendor guarantee, feed
composition, ambient conditions, inlet pressure, uptime and BOG strategy
[E07].

All LNG is stored in ISO tanks. The facility requires BOG
recovery/control, filling metering, overfill protection, ESD, grounding,
hazardous-area control, fire/gas detection, separation distances, spill
impoundment where applicable, road loading and dry-cargo-barge handling.
The terminal does not send bulk LNG through a product hose to the
dry-cargo barge; filled ISO tanks are cargo.

[]

Palmarejo LNG and ISO-tank logistics

15. Produced Water Treatment, Injection and EOR

Produced water leaves the lower section of the main separator and flows
through a treatment train selected to meet injection-water quality. The
conceptual sequence is deoiling/coalescing, flotation or equivalent
polishing, filtration, chemical treatment, storage, high-pressure
injection pumps, metering/header allocation and injection wells.

The project basis is 100% produced-water reinjection, with no routine
disposal to surface pits. Injection targets include Eocene, Paleocene
and basement intervals subject to reservoir plans, containment and
compatibility. The EOR statement reports favorable pressure response and
2026 injection performance near 1.2 MBWIPD versus 1.3 MBWIPD planned,
increasing toward 1.4 MBWIPD. Future facility sizing must also test the
portfolio produced-water envelope, which is materially larger than the
current EOR rates.

[]

Produced-water treatment and EOR injection

Water-injection FEED requires formation-water and produced-water
chemistry; suspended oil/solids limits; oxygen, bacteria and sulfate
strategy; scale/souring/corrosion studies; injectivity/falloff tests;
fracture-pressure margin; well mechanical-integrity tests; annulus
monitoring; VRR targets; reservoir-pressure surveillance and response
plans. Pump discharge pressure shall remain below the approved
containment/fracture limit with high-pressure trip and relief
protection.

16. Crude Storage, LACT, Palmarejo and Lake Export

Crude leaves the oil compartment of the main separator and enters two
EFB tanks whose capacity remains to be defined. The tanks require
working-volume basis, high-high independent overfill protection,
fire/gas detection, containment, water draw-off, vapor management and
transfer-pump suction protection.

A LACT unit is located downstream of EFB storage and before the transfer
pipeline to Palmarejo. The conceptual LACT includes transfer/charge
pumps, strainer, air eliminator, custody-transfer Coriolis meter,
automatic sampler, BS&W and density monitoring, control valve, proving
connection and off-spec diversion. The fiscal boundary, ownership and
uncertainty class require partner agreement.

Palmarejo contains two 80,000-bbl crude tanks. Gross autonomy is
approximately 5.7 days at 27.9 kbopd and 4.0 days at 40 kbopd before
working-volume, heel, off-spec and outage deductions. Storage and
barge-cycle modeling is required to avoid production curtailment.

Crude is transferred from the Palmarejo tanks through export pumps and a
dedicated terminal loading arm/hose to a tank barge. A simple mooring
buoy secures the barge and carries no product. Marine ESD, emergency
release, drip/spill containment, berth/mooring loads, bathymetry,
weather limits, communications and oil-spill response require FEED.
Cabotage from Palmarejo to Puerto Miranda is retained as a contingency
route subject to commercial and regulatory agreement.

[]

Crude LACT and barge export

The project uses three separated modular power systems.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Field / EPFs / wells    Owner-built network;    Protection

                          EPF; HV field feeders   mobile-spare decision,
                          to well transformers    sectionalization,
                          and VFDs.               SCADA, black start and
                                                  fuel-gas continuity.

  Integrated FSB/CPF      Independent normal and  Independent start fuel,
                          backup gas generation   UPS/DC, emergency
                          for compression,        board, selective load
                          separation, pumps,      shedding and
                          treatment, flare        one-train-down study.
                          auxiliaries and

  Palmarejo LNG/export    Existing Palmarejo grid Islanding/stability
                          plus gas gensets for    study, LNG emergency
                          backup, capacity        loads, BOG/control
                          support and black       continuity and terminal
                          start.                  transfer interlocks.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

A normally open emergency intertie between field/EPF and FSB systems may
be evaluated, but a permanently common bus could create an unacceptable
common-mode failure. Palmarejo is not assumed to be powered by FSB.

[]

Project power systems

18. Process Safety, ESD/PSD, Fire Protection and Loss Prevention

Normal operation is multiphase from the well through the EPF and trunk
network to FSB. Safeguarding must therefore prevent one pressure or
containment event from escalating through the connected network. The
conceptual protection layers are: inherent design/MAWP hierarchy; basic
process control and alarms; PSD/ESD isolation; relief/depressuring to
the FSB flare; fire/gas detection and emergency response.

The well, EPF, trunk and FSB are separate isolation zones. High
well/flowline pressure trips the lift equipment and closes the remote
well shutdown valve. Low-low well/lateral pressure or rapid decay
initiates a suspected-rupture response. High EPF manifold or trunk
pressure trips the booster and closes both-end isolation. High-high
slug-vessel level/pressure curtails incoming EPFs in a defined sequence.
High-high main-separator pressure trips compression, restricts inflow
and escalates to ESD/depressuring if pressure continues to rise.

[]

Conceptual ESD and PSD hierarchy

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Initiating        Automatic         Operator action   FEED evidence
  condition         conceptual                          required
                    response                            
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- --------------------
  Well/lateral high Trip VFD/lift,    Confirm           Shut-in pressure,
  pressure          close remote well isolation,        lift deadhead,
                    SSV and alarm     diagnose          transient model,
                    EPF.              downstream        valve closure time.
                                      blockage and      
                                      authorize         
                                      controlled        
                                      restart.          

  Well/lateral      Stop lift, close  Verify leak,      Leak-detection
  low-low pressure  well SSV; assess  isolate local     thresholds and
  or rapid decay    line break.       section, mobilize false-trip analysis.
                                      response.         

  EPF               Trip booster;     Confirm both-end  MAWP hierarchy,
  manifold/trunk    close EPF trunk   isolation and     pressure-wave study
  high-high         ESDV and FSB      manage trapped    and line-pack
  pressure          receiver ESDV.    inventory.        calculation.

  Slug-vessel       Stop liquid       Stabilize         Dynamic
  high-high level   transfer if       separator and     vessel/separator
                    failed state      drain/transfer    model and
                    dictates; curtail under procedure.  cause/effect.
                    EPFs in sequence;                   
                    isolate affected                    
                    trunks.                             

  Confirmed gas or  ESD affected      Muster and        F&G mapping,
  fire at EPF       wells/EPF;        emergency         hazardous-area
                    isolate fuel and  response; no      classification,
                    trunk; trip       intervention      fire-risk study and
                    nonessential      without gas-free  occupied-building
                    power;            confirmation.     siting.
                    alarm/evacuate.                     

  Fire/gas at FSB   Facility ESD,     Evacuate,         HAZOP/LOPA, QRA,
                    sectional         initiate incident flare/depressuring
                    isolation,        command and       and
                    compressor trip   protect adjacent  emergency-response
                    and zoned         systems.          study.

                    flare.                              

  Total power       Fail valves to    Start black-start Load list,
  failure           defined safe      sequence and      stability,
                    positions; trip   controlled        protection and
                    rotating          restart.          black-start study.
                    equipment;                          
                    preserve UPS/DC.                    
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------

The assumption that only gensets can burn is rejected. Credible EPF fire
sources include manifold/valve/flange leaks, multiphase booster seals,
pig launcher connections, fuel-gas skid, temporary test connections, hot
exhausts, electrical arcs, static and lightning. Fire/gas coverage shall
be performance-based. NFPA 59A is applicable to Palmarejo LNG scope, not
as the default EPF firewater design standard.

The need for a conventional firewater arrangement with electric pump,
diesel standby pump and jockey pump shall be established by fire-demand
and layout studies. It is unlikely to be economic or necessary at every
small EPF if separation, isolation, dry-chemical/portable response and
emergency services provide adequate protection; FSB and Palmarejo may
require dedicated firewater systems based on quantified fire scenarios.

[]

Flare, venting and closed drains

Bypass lines are process-routing devices and cannot replace
depressuring. Hydrocarbon maintenance venting is minimized. Isolated
inventory is displaced/recovered where practical; residual low-pressure
gas may use a controlled safe vent or portable flare only after
dispersion, ignition-control, emissions and permit review. FSB process
depressuring is routed to the central flare.

19. Flow Assurance, Production Chemistry, Materials and Integrity

The multiphase concept depends on validated operating envelopes
throughout field life. Principal threats are terrain and hydrodynamic
slugging, pigging slugs, high liquid holdup at decline, excessive
velocity/erosion at peak, gas breakout and pump instability,
wax/asphaltene, hydrates, emulsion/foam, sand, CO₂/H₂S corrosion, scale,
flexible-pipe permeation/rapid-gas decompression and restart after
shutdown.

[]

Flow-assurance threats and controls

The integrated steady-state and transient model shall include individual
wells, local laterals, EPF manifolds/boosters, twelve trunks,
route/elevation profiles, central manifold, slug vessel, separator and
backpressure from the central process. Required cases include minimum,
normal, maximum, decline, one EPF shutdown, compressor trip, full-field
shutdown, restart, pigging, high-water-cut, high-GVF and line-blockage
scenarios.

The pigging philosophy prevents deposit accumulation and line
restriction but can create the maximum liquid surge. Pig campaigns
require controlled pig speed, launch/receive interlocks, pig detection,
receiver capacity, chemical batching if required, slug-vessel routing
and a defined upstream curtailment sequence. The slug vessel shall be
sized to the modelled credible surge with operating margin.

A field sampling and production-chemistry program shall establish wax
appearance temperature, pour point/rheology, asphaltene tendency,
emulsion stability, foaming, scale indices, corrosion, solids/sand,
produced-water chemistry and bacteria. Chemical injection is included as
a provision, not a fixed chemical program. Materials selection shall
cover carbon steel, corrosion allowance/monitoring, inhibitors,
sour-service applicability, flexible-pipe qualification, elastomers, NGL
and cryogenic materials.

20. Environmental, Social, Security and Regulatory Basis

The environmental basis includes no routine produced-water discharge,
control of well-test flaring, no routine atmospheric process venting,
spill containment, historical pit/spill remediation, waste management,
water-injection containment, emissions accounting and lake-terminal
oil-spill response. The 72-hour well-test flare is an operating
requirement but still needs Ministry and environmental approval,
radiation/dispersion controls and gas accounting.

Social constraints affect at least P-108, P-173, P-95, P-180 and P-152.
The development schedule shall not assume access until community
engagement and right-of-way requirements are closed. The PDVSA workbook
also identifies domestic gas-network regularization in Campo La Paz
following ESP implementation.

Security risk includes theft, vandalism, unauthorized access, pipeline
damage and personnel movement. The facility design requires controlled
perimeters, security lighting, communications, access control,
surveillance, line patrol/leak detection and coordination with approved
state-security interfaces while preserving HSE command authority.

A current approval matrix shall identify the Venezuelan ministry,
PDVSA/partner, environmental, land/right-of-way, water injection,
flaring, custody metering, pressure equipment, electrical, hazardous
area, marine and port approvals. International API, IEC, NFPA, NORSOK
and DNV practices support design but do not replace applicable COVENIN
and Venezuelan requirements.

21. Operations, Maintenance, Reliability and Readiness

Each EPF is normally staffed by a control-room operator, one or two
field operators and security personnel. Staffing requires competency
matrices, control-room alarm philosophy, field rounds, permit to work,
simultaneous-operations rules, lockout/tagout, confined-space and
hot-work control, emergency response and fatigue management.

FSB operations require trained process, compression,
electrical/instrument, laboratory, maintenance and emergency-response
staff. Palmarejo adds LNG/cryogenic and marine competencies. Operations
readiness begins during design and includes operating procedures,
cause/effect validation, alarm rationalization, spare parts,
computerized maintenance, FAT/SAT, loop tests, mechanical completion,
pre-startup safety review, commissioning and assisted operation.

The existing FDP targets more than 95% availability. That target is not
yet supported by RAM modeling. The reliability study shall compare
duty-only plus mobile spare, duty/standby and installed parallel
capacity for EPF boosters, central compression, water injection, crude
transfer, LACT and LNG modules. Long-lead spares include ESP/ePCP
assemblies, VFDs, transformers, pump rotors/stators or screws,
compressor components, analyzer spares, ESD valves, generator controls
and flexible-pipe fittings.

22. Export, Marine, Road and Product Logistics

The logistics system has three product routes: crude by tank barge, NGL
by road tanker and LNG by ISO tanks through road and dry-cargo barge
modes. Custody points, product specifications, loading rates, storage
working volumes, turnaround times, fleet size, berth availability,
weather downtime and off-spec handling must be integrated with
production forecasts.

[]

Integrated export logistics

The crude commercial model is sensitive to cargo size. The original FDP
assumes approximately 100,000-bbl barge parcels for early sales; a
requirement to accumulate 500,000-bbl parcels delays self-funding. The
commercial team shall confirm acceptable parcels, title transfer,
pricing, inspection and terminal interfaces before relying on the
schedule.

LNG ISO-tank logistics require a complete cycle model: tank fleet, fill
duration, cooling/heel, inspection, truck supply, road limits, terminal
crane/handling, barge capacity, return cycle, weather and maintenance.
All LNG inventory is in ISO tanks, so production can be constrained by
container availability even when liquefaction capacity is available.

23. Project Execution, Contracting and Modular Deployment

The development follows parallel workstreams but uses strict readiness
gates. Reactivation rigs shall not outrun EPF, line and FSB readiness.
EPF fabrication capacity, well equipment delivery,
flexible-pipe/fittings, transformers/VFDs, pig valves, boosters,
compressors, test packages and LACT equipment require a controlled
procurement schedule.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Workstream                          Required commercial and execution
                                      sequence
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Well services / rigs / lift         Tender → technical/commercial
                                      evaluation → contract → well
                                      AFE/service order → equipment
                                      readiness → intervention → 12-hour
                                      stable-flow rig-release gate →
                                      72-hour test.

  EPFs and field power                Basis/concept → standardized
                                      engineering → vendor
                                      prequalification → AFE → batch
                                      fabrication → FAT → site/civil
                                      readiness → hookup → loop test →
                                      commissioning.

  FSB/CPF and compression             Brownfield survey → capacity/HAZID
                                      → FEED → shutdown/tie-in plan → AFE
                                      → procurement/fabrication →
                                      construction → PSSR → staged
                                      startup.

  Trunklines                          GIS/route and hydraulic study →
                                      material/diameter selection →
                                      right-of-way → AFE →
                                      installation/testing → pigging
                                      baseline → startup.

  Water injection                     Reservoir/injectivity basis → water
                                      specification → treatment/pump FEED
                                      → well integrity → AFE →
                                      commissioning → surveillance.

  Boscan gas export                   Term sheet/specification → route
                                      survey → treatment/compression FEED
                                      → custody interface → AFE →
                                      construction → performance test.

  Palmarejo terminal/LNG              Market/logistics gate → marine and
                                      LNG siting studies → FEED/vendor
                                      guarantees → AFE → staged modules →
                                      terminal commissioning.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The contracting model uses separate MSAs for well services,
EPCM/facilities, compression/gas treatment and other specialized scopes,
integrated by a dedicated EPCM/PMO function. Each project requires an
individual AFE with engineering time, execution time, mobilization, unit
prices, contractor equipment, performance KPIs, downtime/NPT tracking,
QA/QC, incident investigation, failure analysis and replacement
provisions.

24. Capital, Operating Cost and Economics

The original FDP reports total program CAPEX near US$256 million,
including approximately US$70 million for Phase 1 La Paz, US$75 million
for Mara/Mara West, US$40 million for La Paz Sur, US$45 million for El
Moján and US$26 million for gas expansion [S01]. Other tables report
different all-field or post-contract totals. These are planning figures,
not a reconciled Class 3 estimate.

The PDVSA workbook uses US$65/bbl Brent, oil sales around 95% of Brent
and US$1.06/Mcf gas [S03]. These assumptions shall be retained as a
source case and compared with downside/base/upside sensitivities. Costs
must be rebuilt from current vendor RFIs, local quotations, quantities
and execution schedules.

The staged investment logic is retained: early well reactivation, EPFs,
FSB debottlenecking and Boscan pilot; expand only when production and
gas surplus are sustained; defer full NGL recovery and Palmarejo LNG
until composition, market, logistics and vendor guarantees justify
CAPEX. Abandonment, environmental liabilities, import/logistics,
security and foreign-exchange exposure shall be included.

The minimum FEED package includes the approved design basis; process
simulation and heat/material balances; PFDs; preliminary P&IDs;
equipment, line, valve, instrument, relief and tie-in registers; plot
plans and 3D model; HAZID/HAZOP/LOPA/SIL; fire/gas and QRA studies;

model; production chemistry; corrosion/materials; flexible-pipe
qualification; electrical load/short-circuit/stability/black-start
studies; telecommunications/SCADA; water-injection plan; marine/terminal
studies; RAM; operations readiness; execution schedule; Class 3
estimate; and regulatory/approval matrix.

Appendix C. Explicit Open Data Requirements

The following data remain mandatory: controlled 38-well allocation;
minimum/normal/maximum oil, water and gas per well/EPF over field life;
wellhead flowing and shut-in pressures; separator and FSB MAWP; PVT and
current gas/oil/water composition; wax/asphaltene/rheology; sand;
route/elevation surveys; actual pipe internal diameters; pump/compressor
curves; slug/separator geometry; Boscan specification; injection-water
quality and pressure limits; complete electrical load list; EFB tank
capacities; and current ownership/condition of export infrastructure.

Appendix H. PDVSA–PetroUrdaneta Business-Plan Premises and Control Metrics

Premisas

Este Plan de Negocio estima extraer durante el período de evaluación
desde el año 2025 al 2036 (19 de Octubre 2036) una producción de 83,9
MMBls de Petróleo y 167,1 MMMPC de Gas, para ello se clasificaron los
perfiles de producción OPEX y CAPEX en función de las segregaciones
existentes (PCML, MARP y MELIV) de los Campos La Paz, Mara y El Moján.
SUBSUELO
1. Para la evaluación de este proyecto se inició con una producción base
de 1388 BND asociado a 9 pozos categoría 1 (P-016, P-108, P-152, P-173,
P-199A, P-207, P-180, P-179 y P-176) para el cierre Diciembre 2025.
2. Este ejercio considera la reapertura de 102 pozos categoría 2,3,5 y 9
para el paquete de mantenimiento, 38 nuevos pozos a ser perforados
dentro del paquete de crecimiento, 10 estimulaciones,
reacondicionamiento de 3 pozos inyectores de agua existente y la
conversión de 13 pozos inyectores de agua como disposal (Basamento y
Cretáceo).
3. Durante el periodo 2027-2028 se realizará la evaluación de cambio de
método a Sistema de Bombeo Electro Sumergible de 5 pozos piloto del
Campo La Paz, que aportará la información necesaria para la extensión de
la propuesta a partir del año 2029.
4. Para la reactivación de los pozos pertenecientes al yacimiento
EOC/PAL se propone la instalación de equipos de control arena
(rejillas).
5. Para garantizar la actividad de reacondicionamiento de pozos se debe
contar con un Taladro de 750 hp para los pozos asociados a los
yacimientos del cretáceo y basamento y un taladro de 350 hp para los
pozos someros asociados a los yacimientos del EOC PAL.
6. Se estima iniciar la perforación de pozos en el año 2028, para ello
se debe contar con un taladro de 750 hp para los pozos someros y un
taladro de 1500 hp para los pozos profundos.
7. En 2 años se propone evaluar el Plan de Negocio basados en la
evaluación realizada del proyecto de cambio de método a bombeo electro
sumergible implementado en el Campo La Paz y resultados de los EEII
propuestos.
8. Respecto La Paz Sur se estima el inicio de la producción en el 2028;
posteriormente se realizará un estudio integrado para la evaluación.
9. Los Campos Mara y El Moján actualmente se encuentran inactivos, se
estima iniciar producción en el año 2026 con la apertura de 4 pozos
(flujo natural) cuya producción será manejada mediante cabotaje
terrestre hasta EF B de Campo La Paz. Luego del acondicionamiento de la
infraestructura se procederá a la reapertura de los pozos a partir del
año 2029.
10. La estrategia de producción de los pozos del Campo Mara está
condicionada a la evaluación del Proyecto Piloto de Pozos BES del Campo
La Paz, al Estudio Integrado propuesto para el área, y al cumplimiento
de las condiciones de seguridad física y operativa requeridas.
11. Los servicios a pozos para el mantenimiento de producción se
establecieron por método, para bombeo de cavidad progresiva cada 5 años,
para bombeo electrosumergible 2,5 años y 7 años para levantamiento
artificial por gas lift.
12. El sistema de levantamiento artificial por gas es un sistema cerrado
y no requiere de un volumen de gas adicional para su funcionamiento. El
uso del gas de formacion está distribuido a: Gas combustible, venta de
gas a tercero (PDVSA GAS), mermas y perdidas/venteo.
13. Un total de 6 pozos, 4 del Campo Mara (DM-0021, DM-0023, DM-0010 y
DM-123) y 2 del Campo El Moján (DMM-0002 y DMM-003), fueron considerados
para su reapertura (en 2026) y debido a sus características de
producción por despresurización de tubería (intermitente), no fueron
asociados gastos de subsuelo en esta actividad, además el esquema de
producción será por cabotaje terrestre (camión de vacío). No obstante
que para el periodo entre los años 2029 y 2032 se consideró el tendido
de línea de flujo de los pozos mencionados, exceptuando el DMM-003.
14. Las estimaciones del perfil volumétrico requieren una mayor
recolección de datos a fin de reducir las incertidumbres. Se prevé la
realización de actividades para obtener la información necesaria que
permita mejorar los análisis.
15. Se prevén ajustes operativos en bombas y pruebas con rejillas para
optimizar la producción, aunque las estimaciones se basan en datos
preliminares y podrían presentar desviaciones. Las operaciones de
reacondicionamiento implicarán el retiro de liners antiguos, con
posibles riesgos de falla, por lo que se evaluará cada caso de forma
individual.
16. En el área de La Paz Sur, a partir de la interpretación de la línea
sísmica 2D, se tiene la propuesta de perforación de dos (2) pozos
exploratorios con la finalidad de delinear los límites del yacimiento
para someter la incorporación de reservas probadas, extender la
poligonal del área y concebir un posible plan de explotación del área.
17. Debido al comportamiento reciente de los pozos del Campo Mara
Pesado, se ha evidenciado un aumento en la producción de agua atribuible
al avance del frente de agua, así como un reacomodo de la capa de gas en
los yacimientos Cret DM 115 y Cret DM 151, lo que incrementa el nivel de
incertidumbre sobre las condiciones actuales de dichos yacimientos.
Frente a esta incertidumbre, las reservas remanentes establecidas en el
presente perfil serán drenadas con los pozos a reactivar, sin considerar
el drenaje de reservas remanentes asociadas a actividades de
perforación, la cual quedará sujeta a los resultados de los Estudios
Integrados de Yacimientos.
18. Considerando la calidad del gas reflejada en las cromatografías
recientes y los volúmenes a recuperar en el campo La Paz, se establece
la extracción de las fracciones pesadas del gas para su
comercialización, así como la venta del excedente de gas seco
resultante, luego de satisfacer el autoconsumo requerido para
generación.

SUPERFICIE
1. Durante el primer año se debe garantizar la instalación de una unidad
de medición fiscal que permita la medición de la producción.
2. Realizar el tendido de 7057 metros de tubería de 10 pulgadas de
diámetro desde la EF B hasta la trampa El Maluco para mejorar la
confiabilidad operacional y mantener la ruta de entrega de la
producción.
3. Realizar oportunamente mientras se vaya requiriendo los tendidos de
líneas de flujo con tubería flexible de los pozos que se vayan
reactivando tanto en el Campo La Paz como en el Campo Mara.
4. Realizar en el primer año el mantenimiento mayor de tres (3) bombas
reciprocantes, dos (2) para el despacho de crudo y una (1) para el
sistema de agua salada, y paulatinamente el mantenimiento mayor de otras
tres (3) bombas para garantizar el manejo de todos los volúmenes
plasmados en el Plan de Negocio.
5. Iniciar en el año 2027 un sistema de generación Eléctrico
independiente al Sistema Eléctrico Nacional que garantice la
distribución a La Planta Compresora, Estaciones de Flujo y a todos los
pozos.
6. Durante los 2 primeros años se debe gestionar y construir un nuevo
tanque multifuncional de 20 mil barriles en la EF B del Campo La Paz,
que permita en una primera instancia realizar el mantenimiento mayor en
el año 2027 de los tanques activos (1, 2 y 4) en la EF B del Campo La
Paz y luego servir para manejar la producción proveniente del Campo
Mara.
7. Se plantea el manejo de la Producción del Campo Mara Liviano mediante
anillos para el sistema de levantamiento artificial por gas lift y
troncales de recolección de la producción, pero una vez valiado el
proyecto del sistema de levantamiento artificial mediante el sistema BES
en el Campo La Paz, se evaluará la implementación de este sistema en
este campo lo que implicaría una reformación del manejo y distribución
de la Infraestructura.
8. La Gerencia de Coordinación Operacional Deberá ser responsable por la
ejecución de los proyectos de la infraestructura involucrada bajo su
custodia, que garantice la confiablidad de la ruta de entrega de la
producción a partir del patio de Tanque Palmarejo de Mara hasta el
Puerto de Embarque Puerto Miranda. (esto cambiaría cuando se instale la
unidad LACT en la EF B).
9. En vista de la inexistencia de infraestructura adecuada para el
transporte del gas producido por la Empresa Mixta, inicialmente, el
volumen de Gas Producido será reinyectado una vez se compruebe la
viabilidad del proyecto mediante un Estudio Integrado de Yacimientos, al
igual que será utilizado para fines operativos. La entrega y
comercialización a PDVSA Gas de parte o de la totalidad del gas
producido, de igual forma, podrá ser evaluada y está sujeta a la
disponibilidad y adecuación de la infraestructura necesaria, lo cual
depende de la negociación de un acuerdo económico viable para las
partes.
10. Se reactivará la Estación de Flujo K8 en el Campo La Paz como Sala
de Bombeo K8, debido al incremento de la cantidad de pozos que contempla
este Plan para el año 2029.
11. En los Campos La Paz y Mara Pesado se usará la filosofía de bombeo
multifásico y troncales de recolección para el manejo de la producción,
en consecuencia, la Estaciones de Flujo H7, L7, K8 y C de la Segregación
La Paz y MRV1, MRV2 y MRV3 de la Segregación Mara Pesado cambian su
filosofía de operación y pasan a ser Estaciones de Bombeo Multifásico.
12. Se realizará la medición de las variables de los pozos a nivel de
cabezal mediante equipos portátiles de medición multifásico tanto en el
Campo La Paz como en Mara.
13. Para garantizar la atención de las actividades asociadas a
operaciones de producción, mantenimiento, seguridad y resguardo del
personal e instalaciones, SIAHO, servicios generales y administrativos,
en el año 2027 se alquilarán 10 vehículos, considerando las 5 unidades
con las que ya cuenta actualmente la Empresa Mixta. Conforme a las
necesidades, podrán alquilarse más vehículos. Asimismo, a partir del año
2029, se iniciará de manera progresiva la adquisición de 24 unidades
adicionales."
14. Se construirá un múltiple de producción y medida de 20 cupos en la
EF B del Campo La Paz el cual se ejecutará en 2 fases para soportar el
crecimiento de los pozos.
15. En el año 2029 se construirá una planta de tratamiento para el agua
a inyectar en los yacimientos del campo La Paz la cual manejará los
volúmenes de agua de ambos Campos.
16. Basados en la información de los levantamientos actuales en Campo,
se contempla realizar saneamientos y remediación de los pasivos
ambientales existentes en las áreas operacionales.
17. La ejecución de las inversiones puede variar en función de la
conclusión de los acuerdos entre el Socio B y PDVSA.
18. Se construirá una planta de pretratamiento de gas, previo análisis
técnico-económico, con el propósito de extraer las fracciones pesadas
destinadas a la comercialización y de acondicionar el gas seco para su
venta en los puntos de entrega establecidos.
19. Considerando los volúmenes de gas estimados en el perfil de
producción hasta el año 2052, se plantea la construcción de un
gasoducto, que permitan la comercialización del gas seco excedente. El
dimensionamiento definitivo de ambas líneas quedará sujeto a los
estudios de ingeniería correspondientes.
OTROS
1. Las Mesa Técnicas de Trabajo realizadas se basan en la actualización
del perfil de producción, inversiones y gastos 2025-2036 realizado entre
los equipos técnicos de la Empresa Mixta, CVP y Socio B.
2. Los desembolsos asociados a 2026 corresponden a la mejor visión
presupuestaria de la Empresa Mixta.
2. Luego de evaluación de los proyectos de cambio de método a sistema
BES en el Campo La Paz, se estudiará la implantación de proyectos con
las comunidades para regular la red gas doméstico del Campo.
3. Establecer un plan de seguridad integral con el apoyo de los cuerpos
de seguridad del Estado para disminuir y erradicar hurtos y vandalismo
en el área y garantizar la continuidad operativa de la EM PetroUrdaneta.
4. En caso de falla del oleoducto desde Patio de Tanques Palmarejo de
Mara hasta el Terminal de Embarque Puerto Miranda, una de las
posibilidades que se podrá utilizar como contingencia es el cabotaje
marítimo desde el PDT Palmarejo hasta el TDE Puerto Miranda. Asimismo,
deberán analizarse otras opciones logísticas viables.
5. Considerando el perfil volumétrico previsto para el período 2026–2046
de la Empresa Mixta PetroWayuu, dado que dicha producción será
transportada a través de la infraestructura operada actualmente por la
E.M Petrourdaneta S.A, es necesario anticipar posibles limitaciones en
la capacidad el sistema frente al crecimiento proyectado, en este
contexto, se recomienda establecer un Acuerdo de Servicio no solo entre
la EEMM PetroWayuu S.A y Petrourdaneta S.A, sino también con la Gerencia
de Coordinación Operacional, con el fin de evaluar alternativas que
garanticen el uso eficiente de la infraestructura existente y aseguren
que la participación de Petrourdaneta en el transporte resulte técnica y
económicamente favorable. Por último, es necesario que la EM PetroWayuu
garantice la sustitución de su unidad Coriolis ubicada en la EF-B por
una nueva unidad LACT para la correcta medición de los volúmenes en cada
ciclo de bombeo.
6. La elaboración del presupuesto 2026 de PetroUrdaneta fue anterior al
inicio de esta mesa de trabajo. En consecuencia, las actividades
previstas en dicho presupuesto que no hayan sido ejecutadas antes de la
fecha de incorporación formal de Maha a la Empresa Mixta no deberán
considerarse como obligaciones que deban ser asumidas por Maha con
posterioridad a esa fecha.
7. Se debe llevar a cabo un estudio integrado para evaluar el potencial
de recursos de La Paz Sur y las áreas adyacentes. Las inversiones
estarán condicionadas a los resultados de dicho estudio y a la capacidad
de la Empresa Mixta para asegurar los derechos de producción sobre los
yacimientos conectados, lo cual podría requerir la ampliación del límite
actual de la concesión.

The cross-sample range—approximately 0.5–9.75 mol% CO2, 1,060–1,294
BTU/scf and 1.50–3.34 GPM C3+—is large enough to change corrosion,
compression, Boscan export, NGL and LNG pretreatment design.

PetroUrdaneta Field Development Plan

FINAL BASIS OF DESIGN

  Prepared for: PetroUrdaneta / KEO Energy

  Date: May 30, 2026

  Author: MArtin in del Castillo

PRELIMINARY BASIS OF DESIGN DISCLAIMER

  CRITICAL NOTICE: This Field Development Plan (FDP) and the associated
  cost estimates, scheduling timelines, and technical specifications are
  preliminary and based on initial planning data. All parameters are
  subject to direct validation through upcoming site surveys, contractor
  facility integrity assessments, vendor firm quotations, downhole well
  diagnostics, and AFE-level engineering. Figures presented herein are
  classified as FEL Level IV estimates (±30-40% accuracy) and are
  intended to establish the operational framework and strategic gates.
  Actual capital deployment is strictly conditional upon final
  contractor findings and formal AFE approval.

Project Oversight & Asset Description

Asset Overview

  The PetroUrdaneta project is a major oil and gas redevelopment
  concession located in Zulia State, Venezuela, on the western shore of
  Lake Maracaibo [1]. The concession comprises five distinct fields: La
  Paz, La Paz Sur, Mara, Mara Oeste, and El Mojan [1] [2]. These fields
  represent a massive hydrocarbon resource with an estimated Original
  Oil in Place (OOIP/POES) exceeding 8.6 Billion barrels and Original
  Gas in Place (OGIP/GOES) of 4.6 Trillion cubic feet [2].

Current Production & Operational Baseline

  Currently, active operations and production are conducted only from
  the La Paz field [1]. The current asset production baseline is
  approximately 1,300 BOPD of medium-to-heavy crude [1] [2]. The other
  four fields (Mara, Mara Oeste, La Paz Sur, and El Mojan) are currently
  shut-in or inactive, awaiting systematic reactivation and
  redevelopment under this Field Development Plan (FDP).

Table 1.2A — La Paz Field: Complete Well Inventory (32 Wells)

  The following table presents the complete La Paz well inventory — 8
  currently operative wells and 24 shut-in reactivation candidates. Two
  production scenarios are compared:

-   Basic Reactivation: Simple workover only (clean out, tubing change,
    restore gas lift) — no change in artificial lift method or surface
    facilities.

-   Optimized (Modular System): PetroUrdaneta modular philosophy —
    ESP/ESPCP installation, dedicated wellsite package (MP pump, genset,
    SCADA), proper gas handling, and optimized drawdown.

[TABLE]

  Key Insight: A basic reactivation of all 32 wells (simple workovers,
  restore gas lift) would yield only 3,058 BOPD — barely economic. The
  PetroUrdaneta modular system for la Paz delivers 13.500 BOPD from the
  same wells — a 4.9x multiplier — by deploying ESP/ESPCP/PCP technology
  with dedicated power generation, gas handling, and optimized drawdown
  through wellsite packages. The value is not in the wells themselves,
  but in the execution philosophy and surface infrastructure model.

La Paz Phase 1 — Production Build-Up Summary by Reservoir & Lift Method

  The La Paz FDP target of 13,500 BOPD is achieved primarily through the
  ESP wells in the Basamento and Cretaceous reservoirs, which are
  high-productivity, high-PI wells capable of delivering 1,000-2,600
  BOPD each when equipped with electrical submersible pumps. The EOC/PAL
  wells (shallower, lower PI, heavier crude) provide a secondary base
  volume using ESPCP and PCP technology.

  Key Conclusion: The 13,500 BOPD target is driven by 6 ESP wells in the
  Basamento/Cretaceous formations, which alone deliver 11,187 BOPD (83%
  of target). These are deep, high-PI wells (>5 STB/d/psi) with proven
  reservoir quality and significant remaining reserves. The 26 EOC/PAL
  wells provide an additional 3,795 BOPD (28%) as base volume, creating
  a combined theoretical maximum of 13.500 BOPD (Conservative) 14.982
  111%

  The FDP target of 13,500 BOPD is a conservative plateau (90% of
  theoretical maximum) that accounts for natural decline, maintenance
  downtime, ESP run-life replacements, and well availability (~85%
  uptime). Not all 32 wells need to produce at maximum simultaneously.
  Infill drilling will be evaluated during the post-stabilization
  reservoir study (Section 3.1) to offset future decline.

Crude Flow, Gathering & Export Infrastructure

  The existing surface infrastructure and flow path for produced crude
  are structured as follows:

1.  Wellhead Gathering: Active wells in the La Paz field produce
    multiphase fluids (oil, water, and gas) which are routed via
    individual flowlines to local headers.

2.  Flow Station B (FSB): Multiphase fluids are routed to Flow Station
    B, the central gathering and primary separation facility for the La
    Paz field [1]. At FSB, primary separation of gas, oil, and water
    takes place.

3.  Palmarejo Tank Facilities: Partially stabilized crude oil from FSB
    is pumped via a land trunkline to the Palmarejo Tank Facilities

  (storage and shipping terminal) located on the coast [2].

4.  Export to Puerto Miranda: From Palmarejo, the crude is pumped
    through an offshore subsurface pipeline across Lake Maracaibo to the
    Puerto Miranda Terminal on the eastern shore.

5.  Blending & Sales: At Puerto Miranda, PDVSA takes custody of the
    PetroUrdaneta crude and blends it with other regional light/medium
    crudes (such as Mesa 30 or Santa Barbara) to meet export or domestic
    refinery specifications [2].

Gas Gathering, Compression & Utilization

  Gas management is a critical component of the asset’s daily
  operations:

6.  LF-B Gas Compression Plant: Associated gas separated at FSB is
    routed to the LF-B Gas Compression Plant (La Paz Gas Plant) [1].

7.  Compression Infrastructure: LF-B operates Solar Centauro and Ruston
    TB-5006 gas turbine-driven compressors, compressing gas through
    Low-Pressure (50 to 280 psi), Intermediate-Pressure (270 to 536
    psi), and High-Pressure (526 to 1,125 psi) stages, with a total
    plant capacity of 19.5 MMscfd [1].

Gas Utilization Hierarchy:

-   Gas Lift Methodology: The majority of the compressed gas (15.8
    MMscfd) is reinjected into the active wells as the primary
    artificial lift mechanism for the field [1].

-   Fuel Gas: Approximately 3.0 MMscfd is used internally as fuel gas to
    power the Ruston/Solar compression turbines and local field
    generators [1].

-   Local Community & Losses: The remaining gas is allocated to local
    community support (0.4 MMscfd) or accounted for as system
    shrinkage/losses (0.7 MMscfd) [1].

  The five fields of PetroUrdaneta tap into multiple stacked reservoirs,
  ranging from shallow Eocene sands to deep Cretaceous limestones and
  fractured basement rock [2]:

Executive Summary

  PetroUrdaneta will pursue a production-led, modular redevelopment
  strategy to increase total crude production from the current baseline
  of 1,300 BOPD to approximately 40,000 BOPD by Year 4 [1]. The
  development encompasses four distinct fields in the Maracaibo Basin,
  Zulia State, Venezuela: La Paz, Mara / Mara Oeste, La Paz Sur, and El
  Mojan.

  The cash-flow engine of the project is the La Paz field (Phase 1),
  which will be managed on a strict month-by-month control basis to
  achieve first oil through packages by Month 5 (~February 2027), reach
  10,000 BOPD by Month 10 (~July 2027), and attain self-funding at
  plateau by Month 12 (~September 2027). The subsequent expansion fields
  are treated with progressively higher subsurface and operational
  uncertainty and will be managed on a year-by-year basis through strict
  capital allocation decision gates [1] [2].

Dual-Phase Infrastructure Strategy: Modular vs. Definite Facilities ( PDVSA MODEL)

  To protect investor capital and optimize cash flow, the project
  implements a Dual-Phase Infrastructure Strategy:

8.  Early Production Phase (Modular/Hybrid): Rather than constructing
    permanent, centralized processing plants upfront, the project will
    deploy skid-mounted, modular wellsite packages (EPFs) and flexible
    gathering lines. These packages gather, power, boost, and send
    multiphase production directly to the existing Flow Station B (FSB)
    [2]. This allows rapid first-oil and immediate cash flow generation,
    avoiding oversized, expensive upfront investments.

9.  Definite Production Phase (Sized/Permanent): Once production
    declines and reservoir behavior are stabilized and verified
    (typically after 12-18 months of operation), permanent (definite)
    facilities can be properly sized and built, avoiding stranded
    capital.

10. Redeployment Replication: Modular packages are then disconnected,
    refurbished, and redeployed to subsequent fields (Mara, El Mojan, La
    Paz Sur) to replicate this early-cash-flow strategy, while permanent
    or hybrid facilities are evaluated for the mature field.

Development Philosophy

  The PetroUrdaneta development philosophy is built on three core
  tenets:

1.  Existing Infrastructure First: Maximize the utilization of existing
    assets (Flow Station B, existing gas lift networks, and wellheads)
    to minimize initial CAPEX [1].

2.  Modular Production Systems Second: Deploy standardized,
    skid-mounted, leased or modular equipment to establish early
    production and generate cash flow.

3.  Permanent Facilities Last: Construct permanent, centralized
    processing facilities only after the reservoir performance is
    stabilized and the field’s ultimate potential is confirmed.

  The model is specifically designed not to overspend or oversize
  infrastructure, since larger facilities do not accelerate production
  or increase ultimate recovery but do significantly increase capital
  risk and payback periods [2].

  To guide the valuation and economic modeling of the PetroUrdaneta
  concession, the following table consolidates the foundational project
  development assumptions. These assumptions are based on the Master
  Economic Model v4 and reflect the phased, production-led modular
  philosophy of the project [1] [2]:

Post-Stabilization Reservoir Evaluation

  After each field reaches production stability (defined as 6+
  consecutive months of plateau production with <5% monthly decline), a
  comprehensive reservoir evaluation must be conducted to verify optimum
  production and identify secondary/tertiary recovery opportunities.
  This evaluation is a prerequisite before authorizing permanent
  facilities or the next phase of development.

  The post-stabilization reservoir evaluation will include:

+----------+----------------------------+-------------------+---------+

  Critical Principle: No permanent facility will be sized, and no Phase
  2/3/4 field will be authorized for full development, until this
  post-stabilization reservoir evaluation has been completed for the
  preceding phase. This ensures that infrastructure is sized to actual —
  not theoretical — reservoir performance, and that capital is deployed
  only where subsurface data confirms economic viability.

  The expected outcome of this evaluation is a Field Development Plan
  Update (FDP Rev.C) for each field, incorporating:

-   Revised production forecasts based on actual performance (not
    analogues)

-   Water injection implementation plan with injector locations and
    rates

-   Infill drilling campaign design (locations, depths, expected rates,
    AFEs)

-   Permanent facilities sizing based on confirmed plateau rates

-   Updated economic model with actual OPEX, decline rates, and recovery
    factors

Well Inventory

  The primary candidate basis for the La Paz reactivation is a set of 25
  key candidate wells categorized by priority and recommended lift
  mechanism. These wells represent the highest-probability, lowest-risk
  targets for immediate production restoration [1].

Table 6A: Master Well Screening & Prioritization Matrix (La Paz — 25 Candidates)

  The following integrated table combines the well inventory,
  recommended lift method, CAPEX allocation, capital efficiency
  (USD/BOPD), mechanical complexity, subsurface risk scoring, and final
  prioritization ranking for all La Paz candidate wells. Wells are
  ranked by Screening Score to optimize the allocation of capital and
  rigs [1] [2].

[TABLE]

[TABLE]

  Screening Methodology: Wells are scored on a 0-4 scale combining
  mechanical complexity, subsurface risk, and capital efficiency. Lower
  scores indicate lower risk and higher priority. The ranking optimizes
  rig allocation by sequencing low-risk, high-return wells first
  (Base/Optimization and Tier 1 ESPCP), followed by high-value ESP
  wells, and finally diagnostic wells that require subsurface evaluation
  before commitment [1] [2].

+-------+-----------+----+----+------+----------+-------------------+

1.  ESP vs. ESPCP Evaluation

  The project is actively evaluating the transition of high-productivity
  wells from traditional ESP (Electrical Submersible Pumps) to ESPCP
  (Electrical Submersible Progressing Cavity Pumps). ESPCP combines the
  high-volume downhole motor of an ESP with the positive displacement
  pump of a PCP, making it ideal for the Maracaibo Basin’s viscous,
  gassy, and sandy crudes [2].

  Table 8B: Technical Comparison — ESP vs. ESPCP

+----------+------------+---------------+----------------------------+
|   Metric |   T        |   ESPCP       |   Selection Rationale for  |
|          | raditional |               |   PetroUrdaneta            |
|          |   ESP      | (Alternative) |                            |
+==========+============+===============+============================+
|   V      |   Poor (<  |   Excellent   |   Maracaibo crude is       |
| iscosity |   100 cP)  |   (< 5,000    |   highly viscous; ESPCP    |
|   T      |            |   cP)         |   prevents severe head     |
| olerance |            |               |   degradation.             |
+----------+------------+---------------+----------------------------+
|   Gas    |   Poor     |   Excellent   |   High GOR in              |
|          |   (gas     |   (handles up |   Cretaceous/EOC; ESPCP    |
| Handling |   locking  |   to 40% GVF) |   avoids gas-locking.      |
|          |   at       |               |                            |
|          |            |               |                            |
|          |   >15%     |               |                            |
|          |   GVF)     |               |                            |
+----------+------------+---------------+----------------------------+
|   San    |   Very     |   Excellent   |   Sand production is       |
| d/Solids |   Poor     |   (handles up |   common in PAL/EOC; ESPCP |
|   T      |   (rapid   |   to 5%       |   increases run life.      |
| olerance |   wear)    |   solids)     |                            |
+----------+------------+---------------+----------------------------+
|   System |   Medium   |   High        |   Reduces fuel gas/diesel  |
|   Ef     |   (35-45%) |   (60-70%)    |   consumption for wellsite |
| ficiency |            |               |   generators.              |
+----------+------------+---------------+----------------------------+
|   Power  |   High     |   Medium      |   Lowers wellsite          |
|   Demand |            |   (typically  |   generator sizing and     |
|          | (typically |   80-120 kW)  |   power CAPEX.             |
|          |   150-250  |               |                            |
|          |   kW)      |               |                            |
+----------+------------+---------------+----------------------------+
|   Motor  |   High     |   Low (100 -  |   Lower RPM significantly  |
|   Speed  |   (3,000 - |   500 RPM via |   reduces mechanical wear  |
|          |   3,600    |               |   and increases run life.  |
|          |   RPM)     |   gearbox)    |                            |
+----------+------------+---------------+----------------------------+
|   Run    |   12 - 18  |   24 - 36     |   Reduces costly workover  |
|   Life   |   months   |   months      |   interventions in         |
|          |            |               |   Venezuela.               |
| (Target) |            |               |                            |
+----------+------------+---------------+----------------------------+
|          |   High     |   Medium-High |   12% CAPEX saving per     |
|  Initial |   (US$     |   (US$ 1.85   |   well on equipment.       |
|   CAPEX  |   2.12 MM  |   MM loaded)  |                            |
|          |   fully    |               |                            |
|          |   loaded)  |               |                            |

Mobile Gas Compression for ESP Annular Gas Recovery

  When ESPs or ESPCPs are installed, gas accumulates in the
  casing-tubing annulus, causing casing pressure to build up. This
  backpressure suppresses the fluid level, reducing pump submergence and
  risking pump run-dry or gas-locking. To mitigate this, the project
  will deploy Mobile Gas Compressor Units at ESP clusters:

-   Equipment: Trailer-mounted, rotary screw compressor (0.5 MMscfd
    capacity, 150 HP gas engine) [2].

-   Operation: Automatically draws gas from the well annulus, drawing
    pressure down to <50 psi, and compresses it to 250 psi to inject
    directly into the fuel gas header.

-   Cost: US$ 280,000 per unit (CAPEX under MSA-4).

-   Benefit: Recovers valuable fuel gas, reduces wellsite emissions, and
    increases ESP run life by maintaining optimal submergence.

-   Downhole Monitoring Synergy: Downhole sensor data from ESP systems
    will provide valuable information for validating casing flow
    conditions and ensuring reliable operation within the optimal pump
    operating envelope. This reinforces the benefit of using these
    compression systems on the casing/annulus side, as real-time
    pressure and temperature data enables proactive compression
    scheduling and prevents gas-lock events before they impact
    production.

Cluster Strategy

  To optimize power generation, fluid gathering, and pipeline routing,
  wells are grouped into 12 clusters/wellsites, each served by a
  dedicated wellsite package (EPF) [1] [2]. This strategy implements
  dedicated packages for ESP wells (maximum 1 ESP per package due to
  high power draw) and cluster packages for PCP, Gas Lift, and Rod Pump
  wells.

Modular EPF Strategy

  The modular wellsite packages (EPFs) are designed as standardized,
  skid-mounted, non-processing facilities. They do not perform phase
  separation or water treatment; instead, they gather, protect, boost,
  power, monitor, and send multiphase production directly to Flow
  Station B [2].

Table 10A: Wellsite Package Specifications, Sizing & Cost Summary (Fuel Gas / Dual Fuel)

[TABLE]

1.  Sizing and Engineering Concept of Wellsite Packages

  Each wellsite package is engineered to accommodate a specific number
  of wells within its cluster. Rather than installing individual power
  and pumping facilities at each wellhead, the package centralizes these
  utilities [2]:

-   Multiphase Pump (MPP): High-gas-volume fraction (GVF) twin-screw
    pumps handle the entire multiphase stream (oil, water, gas) without
    requiring separation at the wellsite, pumping directly to FSB [1].

-   Safety Devices: Automated High-Integrity Pressure Protection Systems
    (HIPPS) and emergency shutdown (ESD) valves connected to SCADA
    prevent overpressuring the flexible gathering lines.

-   Instrumentations: Multi-phase flow meters (MPFM) and automated
    chokes allow continuous monitoring of individual well performance
    without permanent test separators.

  Key Design Principle: Each wellsite package is a fully integrated,
  self-contained production module. By centralizing power generation and
  multiphase pumping at the package level, the project avoids the high
  capital cost of individual wellhead utilities. All safety and
  instrumentation devices are integrated onto the single steel skid,
  allowing for rapid deployment, hookup, and eventual redeployment to
  other fields as part of the modular replication strategy [2].

  Wellsite Package Cost Attribution: Rather than attributing facilities
  costs per well tier, the cost of the wellsite package and associated
  equipment (test plant, civil, flexible flowlines, field gas flexpipe
  headers) is attributed as a percentage to each well within the
  cluster. This is because each wellsite package energizes and serves a
  specific cluster of wells (PCPs, rod/gas lift, or ESPs) [2].
