  Design Notes: (1) All generators are Fuel Gas / Dual Fuel — run on
  field gas with diesel backup for start-up or gas interruption. (2) All
  packages include 2 generators at 100% capacity for duty/standby
  alternation. (3) Each generator has a dedicated Gas Conditioning Skid.
  (4) ESP package includes casing gas compressor for annular gas
  evacuation. (5) GL package includes gas lift compressor + TEG
  dehydration. (6) All packages include MPFM (Roxar 2600) for
  fiscal-quality measurement. (7) All packages include HIPPS (SIL-3).
  (8) Costs are equipment + materials only; installation labor is MSA-2
  EPCM scope. (9) Downhole equipment (ESP pump, PCP pump, tubing) is in
  the Well Reactivation AFE (Section 17). (10) OPEX Advantage: Using
  field gas eliminates

  ~$8,500/day diesel cost per package. (11) Cost/Well and Efficiency
  data previously shown in Table 10G are now integrated into Table 10A
  above.

Testing Philosophy

  To minimize upfront capital and avoid duplicate equipment, the project
  excludes permanent test separators at individual wellsites. The
  testing philosophy is structured around three distinct functions: well
  testing after recompletion, custody/allocation metering, and fiscal
  export measurement [2].

+-------+-----+--------+------------------+----------+-------+-------+

  Testing Workflow:

1.  After Recompletion (Day 1): The mobile well test plant is connected
    to the newly completed well via temporary flowlines. A full 24-hour
    flow test is conducted to establish initial production rate (IP),
    water cut, GOR, and flowing tubing/casing pressures. Results are
    used to confirm artificial lift sizing and update the decline
    forecast.

2.  Ongoing Allocation: The Roxar multiphase ultrasonic meter is rotated
    between clusters to provide continuous, non-intrusive production
    allocation without requiring well shutdowns or flow diversions.

3.  Fiscal Export: The LACT unit downstream of FSB provides continuous
    fiscal-grade measurement of all exported crude, ensuring accurate
    royalty and sales volume accounting.

  All production from the clusters is routed as multiphase fluids (oil +
  water + gas commingled) through flexible gathering lines and
  trunklines, and processed centrally at Flow Station B (FSB). No phase
  separation occurs at the wellsite level [2].

Flow Station B Upgrade Roadmap

  Flow Station B (FSB) is the central processing hub for the entire
  PetroUrdaneta development. It must be upgraded in stages to match the
  production ramp-up [1] [2].

Table 12A: Flow Station B Phased Upgrades

Gas Strategy & Monetization

  Gas is managed under a strict hierarchy: Fuel Gas first, Gas Export
  second, Reinjection third, and Gas-to-Wire last [1] [2].

Gas Export Pipeline & Campo Boscan Interconnection

  To fulfill the gas export potential to Campo Boscan (Chevron), a
  dedicated midstream gas export project is planned:

-   Pipeline Specification: 22 km, 8-inch carbon steel pipeline (API 5L
    X52), buried, with external anti-corrosion coating [2].

-   Capacity: Sized for up to 30 MMscfd gas export.

-   Compression: 1x 2-stage reciprocating compressor package (1,200 HP,
    gas-engine driven) located at FSB.

-   Infrastructure: Inlet/outlet headers, TEG dehydration unit (to meet
    Chevron water-dew-point specs), and fiscal custody-transfer
    metering.

Modular Package Redeployment Strategy

  To minimize total program CAPEX, the project treats wellsite packages
  as movable, temporary assets. Once a cluster’s production is
  stabilized and connected to permanent gathering lines, the modular
  package is disconnected, refurbished, and redeployed to the next field
  [2].

1.  Reliability, Spare Parts & Long Lead Items Strategy

  To guarantee an operational uptime of >95% across all modular packages
  and wellsites, the project implements a rigorous Reliability and Spare
  Parts Strategy. Given the geographical and supply chain constraints of
  operating in Zulia State, Venezuela, relying on standard
  “just-in-time” parts delivery is a high-risk approach that would lead
  to extended well downtime and deferred production [2].

  capital efficiency, these items are provided and maintained by the
  respective contractors under the four MSA frameworks. However, to
  ensure immediate availability and align incentives, they are partially
  billed to the operator through a structured mobilization and standby
  rate agreement [1] [2]:

Operational & Commercial Rationale:

1.  Contractor-Held Inventory (The MSA Advantage): By mandating that the
    primary contractors under MSA-1, MSA-2, MSA-3, and MSA-4 maintain
    these critical spares in their local Maracaibo warehouses, the
    operator avoids the full upfront capital expenditure of carrying
    millions of dollars in inventory. The contractors leverage their
    global supply chains and regional hubs (e.g., Houston, Colombia,
    Trinidad) to maintain these stock levels [1].

2.  Partial Billing Structure: To compensate contractors for the capital
    tied up in holding these long-lead items exclusively for
    PetroUrdaneta, the project pays a standby/insurance rate (typically
    25% to 40% of the amortized equipment cost). If a spare part is
    deployed, the remaining balance is billed as an active CAPEX event
    under the respective well’s AFE. This ensures that the contractor is
    incentivized to maintain high-quality, ready-to-deploy equipment
    [2].

3.  Preventive Maintenance (PM) Integration: To prevent catastrophic
    failures, the field operations team under the O&M Manager conducts
    bi-weekly vibration analysis, thermographic imaging, and oil
    sampling on all active MPPs and gensets. If early degradation is
    detected, the standby spare is mobilized and swapped during a
    scheduled 12-hour maintenance window, avoiding unplanned shutdowns
    [2].

Capital Allocation and Decision Gates

  Capital is released in phases, controlled by strict Investment Gates
  tied to production and gas milestones. This prevents over-investment
  in permanent facilities before reservoir potential is proven [1] [2].

1.  EPCM Cost Breakdown & Facility Execution Strategy

  EPCM (Engineering, Procurement, Construction & Management) costs are
  managed under two distinct strategies: Fixed/Permanent Facilities
  (high EPCM load due to civil/site-specific engineering) and
  Hybrid/Modular Facilities (low EPCM load due to standardized,
  repeatable skid designs) [2].

Water Reinjection Strategy & Facility Upgrades

  To comply with Venezuelan environmental regulations and maintain
  reservoir pressure in the EOC/PAL and Cretaceous formations, the
  project adopts a 100% water reinjection strategy. No produced water
  will be discharged to surface pits or evaporation ponds [2].

  Injection patterns will be optimized based on injector well integrity
  considerations and reservoir injectivity to ensure produced water
  provides effective areal sweep efficiency while targeting an optimal
  voidage replacement ratio (VRR), thereby maximizing ultimate recovery
  within each pattern. The VRR target will be maintained between 0.9 and
  1.1 during the early production phase, with adjustments made based on
  real-time pressure monitoring and periodic interference testing
  between injector-producer pairs.

Palmarejo Terminal & Barge Export CAPEX

  To bypass midstream pipeline constraints, the project includes the
  construction of a dedicated barge terminal at Palmarejo [2]:

-   Pipeline Repair: Repairing 4.2 km of the existing main line to
    Palmarejo: US$ 1,200,000 (CAPEX under MSA-2).

-   Terminal Storage: 2x 50,000 BBL carbon steel storage tanks, transfer
    pumps, and custody transfer LACT meters: US$ 4,500,000

  (CAPEX under MSA-2).

-   Jetty & Loading: Loading dock, loading arms, mooring dolphins, and
    marine safety systems: US$ 1,800,000 (CAPEX under MSA-2).

-   Total Terminal CAPEX: US$ 7,500,000.

Operating Expenditure (OPEX) & SG&A

IPM & Backoffice Structure

  The PetroUrdaneta project operates under an Integrated Project
  Management (IPM) contract model, where the operator (Maha Energy)
  provides comprehensive project management, technical oversight, and
  corporate backoffice support to the field operations [1]. The IPM
  structure is designed to be lean, scalable, and fully transparent in
  its cost allocation to the project.

  The IPM structure is organized into two distinct cost layers:

1.  IPM Backoffice Support (US$ 141,500/month): Provides dedicated
    technical specialists (Reservoir Engineer, Geologist, Artificial
    Lift Specialist, Drilling & Completion Specialist, Facilities
    Engineer), remote production real-time monitoring, simulation
    software licenses, plus operational support (Cost Control, PMO,
    Logistics, HSE) and a consolidated corporate overhead & IT/Admin
    lump sum. These resources are shared across the project and
    allocated on a part-time or full-time basis depending on project
    phase. Detailed in Table 17A below.

2.  Field Operations Team (US$ 180,500/month): Direct owner-team
    positions permanently located in Maracaibo — COO, O&M Manager,
    Drilling Manager, Technical Manager, Reservoir Engineering Manager,
    Infrastructure Manager, HSE Manager, Social Responsibility Manager,
    PMO Lead, Admin Support, and Executive Secretary. Detailed in Table
    17B below.

Organizational Chart — Reporting Structure

  The following organizational chart defines the full reporting
  hierarchy for the Maha Business Unit in Venezuela (Maha + PU). The
  structure distinguishes between Maha direct-hire positions (Operations
  Maha) and PU Secondments — Maha staff who are seconded into
  PetroUrdaneta to fulfill operational roles within the joint venture.
  Positions with PU secondments are highlighted in orange.

[]

  Note on PU Secondments: The Technical Manager (Alan McKeon),
  Infrastructure & Project Manager (Juan Conde), and PU
  Operations/Maintenance Manager (Felix Valderrama) are Maha employees
  seconded into PetroUrdaneta. Their costs are charged to the project
  through the IPM backoffice structure. HSE (Mara) and
  Finance/Procurement PU (Lila) are also secondment positions. Security
  is outsourced to a services provider (TBD).

  Note on Lean Structure: There are no in-house Production Engineers or
  Facilities Engineers. Production optimization is managed directly by
  the O&M Manager, and all facilities engineering, construction
  supervision, and commissioning support is provided by the EPCM
  contractor under MSA-2, supervised by the Infrastructure Manager.

  Note: Technical specialists (Reservoir Engineer, Geologist, Artificial
  Lift, Drilling & Completion, Facilities) are allocated on a part-time
  (0.5 FTE) basis during Phase 1. As the project scales to Phases 2-4,
  these positions may transition to full-time (1.0 FTE) with
  proportional cost increase. Remote Production Real-Time Monitoring
  includes a dedicated engineer plus cloud-based SCADA platform
  subscription for 24⁄7 surveillance and optimization. Simulation
  Software covers all subsurface and production engineering licenses
  required for reservoir management and well optimization. Corporate
  Overhead & IT/Admin is a fixed lump sum that covers all remaining
  corporate functions not itemized above.

SG&A Personnel Cost Model

  The field operations are supported by a lean owner organization.
  During the first 6 months, all managers operate on rotation only.
  After 6 months, all positions become permanently resident (no more
  rotation). The COO is based in Caracas [1].

  Engineers. Production optimization is managed directly by the O&M
  Manager, and all facilities engineering, construction, and
  commissioning support is provided by the EPCM contractor under MSA-2,
  supervised by the Infrastructure Manager. This lean owner-team model
  minimizes fixed SG&A while leveraging contractor scalability.

Strategic Comparison: PetroUrdaneta Modular Model vs. PDVSA Traditional Model

  The primary driver of PetroUrdaneta’s success is not a change in
  reservoir understanding, but a fundamental shift in the project
  execution and contracting model. While the reservoir potential has
  always existed, the PDVSA traditional approach has consistently failed
  to unlock it due to sequential execution, oversized infrastructure,
  and front-loaded capital requirements that never generate returns
  within a reasonable timeframe. The PetroUrdaneta modular and hybrid
  system delivers production organically and incrementally, with each
  barrel of oil produced funding the next phase of development [1] [2].

Production Ramp-Up Comparison

  The most critical difference between the two models is the time to
  first oil and the production build-up profile. The PDVSA model
  requires all infrastructure to be completed before a single barrel
  flows; the PetroUrdaneta model produces oil within months of FID.

Table 18A: Production Ramp-Up Timeline Comparison

+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+
|   T     | PDVSA Traditional   | PetroUrdaneta Modular  |             |
| imeline | Model (BOPD)        | Model (BOPD)           |  Difference |
+=========+=====================+========================+=============+
|   Month | 0                   | 1,300 (baseline)       |   PU        |
|   0     |                     |                        |   already   |
|   (FID) |                     |                        |   producing |
+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+
|   Month | 0 (still in         | 1,300 (baseline +      |   +1,300    |
|   3     | engineering)        | first workovers)       |             |
+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+
|   Month | 0 (still in         | 2,500 (first packages  |   +2,500    |
|   5     | procurement)        | online)                |             |
+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+
|   Month | 0 (still in         | 5,000 (Batch 1 + 2     |   +5,000    |
|   6     | procurement)        | ramping)               |             |
+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+
|   Month | 0 (still in         | 8,000 (all packages    |   +8,000    |
|   8     | construction)       | online)                |             |
+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+

|   10    | construction)       | achieved)              |             |

|   12    | commissioning)      | plateau)               |             |
+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+

|   18    | commissioning       | start)                 |             |
|         | trials)             |                        |             |
+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+
|   Month | 2,000-5,000 (first  | 22,000 (Mara plateau)  |   +17,000   |
|   24    | oil, ramp-up)       |                        |   to        |
|         |                     |                        |   +20,000   |
+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+

+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+

|         |                     |                        |   +20,000   |
+---------+---------------------+------------------------+-------------+

  Key Insight: The PDVSA model takes 18-24 months to produce its first
  barrel of oil. The PetroUrdaneta modular model achieves 10,000 BOPD in
  Month 10 — before the PDVSA model has even completed commissioning.
  This means the PetroUrdaneta model generates 12-18 months of
  cumulative revenue before the PDVSA model produces a single dollar.

CAPEX Expenditure Profile Comparison

  The PDVSA model requires massive upfront capital commitment
  (“front-loaded CAPEX”) with no revenue offset for 2+ years. The
  PetroUrdaneta model deploys capital incrementally and becomes
  self-funding before the heaviest CAPEX phases (drilling, permanent
  facilities).

  Key Insight: The PDVSA model reaches cumulative breakeven at
  approximately Month 42-48 (3.5-4 years). The PetroUrdaneta modular
  model reaches self-funding at Month 12-18 and generates positive
  cumulative cash flow by Month 18. The total capital at risk at any
  point is dramatically lower.

OPEX Structure Comparison

  The PDVSA model carries a heavy fixed OPEX burden from Day 1 (large
  permanent staff, centralized facilities maintenance, grid power costs)
  regardless of production level. The PetroUrdaneta model has a variable
  OPEX structure that scales with production.

Conclusion: Why the Modular & Hybrid Model is the Only Viable Solution

  The traditional centralized model is structurally unviable in the
  current Venezuelan operating environment due to:

1.  Capital constraints: Upfront funding of US100 +
    Millionisunavailableorcarriesanextremelyhighcostofcapital.Noinvestorwillcommit100
    MM with a 24-month wait for first oil in Venezuela.

2.  Logistical bottlenecks: Importing large-scale equipment takes 18-24
    months and is subject to sanctions, customs, and port congestion.

3.  Subsurface uncertainty: Historic well data is unreliable after
    decades of deferred maintenance. Oversizing permanent facilities is
    almost certain.

4.  Political risk: A 3-4 year execution timeline exposes the project to
    multiple political cycles and regulatory changes.

5.  No organic growth: The PDVSA model produces zero revenue for 2+
    years, creating a binary outcome (success or total loss) rather than
    an incremental, de-risked growth path.

  The PetroUrdaneta Modular & Hybrid Model eliminates these risks by:

-   Generating first oil at Month 5 and 10,000 BOPD by Month 10

-   Achieving self-funding by Month 12-18 (no further external capital
    required)

-   Growing production organically — each well validates the next
    investment

-   Maintaining redeployment optionality — if any field disappoints,
    assets move to the next opportunity

-   Operating with a lean, cohesive team that has worked together before
    and can execute at speed

  The result is a project that delivers 40,000 BOPD within 4 years at
  approximately 50% of the CAPEX and 40-55% of the OPEX of the
  equivalent PDVSA model, with dramatically lower risk and earlier
  returns [1] [2].

Key Risks and Mitigations

  The project has identified several critical operational, subsurface,
  and midstream risks. Importantly, several key risks have already been
  pre-mitigated through advance planning: the core operations team has
  been secured and has prior working history together (eliminating
  talent and cohesion risk), and a minimum of 4 qualified vendors per
  MSA have been pre-identified (eliminating single-vendor concentration
  risk) [1] [2].

Midstream Export Constraints & Palmarejo Barge Terminal Strategy

  A critical bottleneck exists in the crude export infrastructure [2]:

-   Main Line to Palmarejo: The existing pipeline has several active
    leaks, limiting throughput to 10,000 BOPD until repairs are
    completed.

-   Subsurface Line to Puerto Miranda: There is no current integrity
    evaluation on this line, and it has never been pigged, representing
    an extreme environmental and operational risk.

-   The Barge Export Strategy: To bypass these risks, the project will
    establish a terminal at Palmarejo and use 100,000 BBL barges to
    deliver crude to Puerto Miranda.

    -   Barge Lease Cost: US$ 600,000 per month (US$ 7.2 MM/year OPEX
          under MSA-1).

    -   Total Fluvial OPEX: US$ 9.0 Million/year (including fuel, crew,
          and port fees).

    -   Terminal CAPEX: US$ 7.5 Million (rebuilding Palmarejo tanks,
          jetty, and loading arms).

Project Execution Timeline

  The project execution is organized into three distinct phases defined
  by the D-Day (D0) framework:

-   D0 = PDVSA Contract Signature & Formal Operational Transition (the
    moment Maha/PetroUrdaneta assumes formal operational control)

-   D- (D-minus) = The 2-3 month period before D0, from Term Sheet
    signature through PDVSA contract finalization. During this period,
    critical preparatory activities are executed in parallel with
    legal/regulatory processes to eliminate dead time.

-   D+ (D-plus) = The full operational execution phase after D0, when
    the project has formal authorization to intervene wells, fabricate
    packages, and produce oil.

  This framework ensures that no time is wasted during the 2-3 month
  contract negotiation period with PDVSA. By the time D0 arrives, the
  project team is fully mobilized, site surveys are complete, MSAs are
  negotiated, and fabrication orders are ready to be released — enabling
  immediate execution on Day 1 of formal operations.

Figure 19D-1: D- Phase Gantt Chart (Term Sheet to PDVSA Contract — Apr-Sep 2026)

  Phase activities. These activities do not require formal PDVSA

  negotiation period. Critical path items (Long-Lead Equipment
  Pre-Orders and PDVSA Contract Finalization) are highlighted in red.

  Strategic Advantage of the D- Phase: By executing 2-3 months of
  preparatory work during D-, the project effectively “compresses” the
  post-D0 timeline. On the day of PDVSA contract signature, the team is
  already mobilized, vendors are pre-qualified, MSAs are ready to
  activate, long-lead items are reserved, and environmental permits are
  in process. This means first oil can be achieved within 3.5-4 months
  of D0 (not 5-6 months), because the typical 2-3 month pre-execution
  dead time has been eliminated.

  Note: The detailed Phase 1 execution timeline with activity
  descriptions, durations, parallel operations, and deliverables is
  presented in Table 19D-2 above. The key insight is that wellsite
  packages are standardized, modular, skid-mounted units (not
  custom-engineered permanent facilities), enabling compressed
  procurement cycles and parallel batch assembly. Since contractors
  pre-procured components during the D- Phase, packages are assembled
  and tested in just 2 weeks per batch, allowing immediate hookup as
  soon as well interventions are completed.

Contracting Strategy — Four MSA Framework

  The project utilizes a Four Master Service Agreement (MSA) framework
  to ensure capital discipline. A minimum of 4 pre-qualified vendors
  have been identified for each MSA, providing competitive tension,
  backup capacity, and negotiating leverage [1]:

-   MSA-1: Well Services & EPF (4 vendors): Workovers, PCP/ESP
    installation, and wellsite package fabrication.

-   MSA-2: EPCM (4 vendors): Flow Station B upgrades, permanent
    pipelines, and terminal construction.

-   MSA-3: Drilling (4 vendors): New infill wells and sidetracks.

-   MSA-4: Compression (4 vendors): Annular gas compressors and gas
    export compression.

  Every activity is triggered by an individual, AFE-approved Service
  Order, ensuring that capital is only spent when there is a clear path
  to crude return. The multi-vendor approach per MSA eliminates
  single-vendor dependency risk and enables immediate reallocation of
  work packages if any contractor underperforms [2].

Parallel Operations & Fast-Track Execution Scheduling

  To achieve the 10,000 BOPD target within 8 weeks of D0, the project
  executes multiple workstreams in parallel:

-   Rig operations run concurrently with wellsite package assembly.

-   Flow Station B upgrades occur in parallel with well interventions.

-   Environmental permitting is conducted during the D- Phase to
    eliminate dead time.

-   Downhole equipment procurement is completed during D-2
    (pre-contract) so that workovers can begin immediately at D0+1 week.

Comprehensive Project Constraints

  The project is governed by 12 critical constraints [2]:

1.  Degraded Facilities: FSB and existing pipelines require extensive
    rehabilitation.

2.  Well Integrity: Casing corrosion and cement failure require
    proactive CBL logging.

3.  Physical Security: Asset theft in Zulia State requires the Lean
    Security Program.

4.  National Infrastructure: Grid blackouts require 100% self-generation
    at wellsites.

5.  Chevron Gas Contract: Gas export is conditional upon meeting Chevron
    specifications.

6.  Operational Transition: Handover of operational control from the
    previous operator.

7.  Master Service Agreements: Long-term MSAs are required to secure
    rigs and services.

8.  Environmental Liabilities: Remediation of historical pits and
    spills.

9.  Critical Equipment Lead Times: ESPs and compressors have 5-6 month
    lead times.

10. Regulatory Timelines: Ministry and PDVSA approvals require proactive
    management.

11. Local Labor Relations: Union interface requires dedicated local
    community managers.

12. FX & Inflation: Currency volatility requires US$-denominated
    contracts and local banking structures.

  Total D- Phase Investment (Pre-Contract): ~US$ 27.7 Million — This
  includes team salaries (0.99M), manifold EPCM design & installation
  (1.1M), plus all La Paz downhole equipment & package fabrication
  orders ($25.47M) placed in D-2 to ensure immediate delivery and
  schedule compliance. Equipment POs are conditional and partially
  cancellable if D0 does not materialize. Equipment POs are conditional
  and partially cancellable if D0 does not materialize (cancellation
  fees 15-20%).

  Total D+ Phase 1 CAPEX (La Paz only, Post-Contract): ~US$ 45.6 Million
  — Deployed aggressively in the first 8 months, driven by parallel
  batch fabrication of 12 packages and continuous workover campaign.

  Total All-Fields CAPEX (D-2 to D+30): ~US$ 113.6 Million — Includes La
  Paz (32 wells, 12 packages), Mara (16 wells), Mara Oeste (16 wells),
  La Paz Sur (16 wells), and El Mojan (16 wells) = 96 wells total. Mara
  and other fields utilize redeployed wellsite packages from La Paz,
  eliminating new fabrication costs and optimizing total program capital
  efficiency.

  Peak Monthly CAPEX: US$ 11.58 Million (December 2026, D+7) — Coincides
  with Batch 3 fabrication + active rig campaign + multiple package
  installations.

  Key Principle: LACT unit, all 12 wellsite packages, well equipment
  (ESP/PCP/GL/RP downhole sets), flexlines, and artificial lift systems
  must be purchased and fabrication started immediately in D-2 to ensure
  delivery within the target timeline. This front-loads ~70% of total
  CAPEX into the pre-contract phase, ensuring 100% material readiness at
  D0.

  (September 2026 – December 2026).

Critical Commercial Constraint: Oil Sales in Smaller Batches

  IMPORTANT: Self-funding is contingent upon the ability to sell crude
  oil in batches significantly smaller than 1,000,000 barrels. At 10,000
  BOPD, accumulating 1 million barrels requires approximately 100 days
  of production — meaning the first cargo sale would not occur until
  Month 7+ post-first-oil if limited to standard VLCC-sized parcels.

  The Operations team confirms its ability to deliver the production
  volumes as scheduled. However, the self-funding timeline assumes the
  External Affairs Director and commercial team successfully negotiate
  one or more of the following crude sales mechanisms:

+-------------------+------------+--------------+-------+------------+
|   Sales Mechanism |   Batch    |   Frequency  |       |   N        |
|                   |   Size     |   at 10K     |  Cash | egotiation |
|                   |            |   BOPD       |       |   Status   |
|                   |            |              | Cycle |            |
+===================+============+==============+=======+============+
|   Barge sales     |   100,000  |   Every 10   |       |   To be    |
|   (Palmarejo      |   BBL      |   days       | 15-20 |            |
|   terminal)       |            |              |       | negotiated |
|                   |            |              |  days |            |
+-------------------+------------+--------------+-------+------------+
|   Spot market     |   200,0    |   Every      |       |   To be    |
|   (small parcels) | 00–300,000 |   20-30 days | 20-30 |            |
|                   |   BBL      |              |       | negotiated |
|                   |            |              |  days |            |
+-------------------+------------+--------------+-------+------------+
|   PDVSA blending  |   As       |   Continuous |       |   Existing |
|   (Puerto         |            |              | 30-60 |   a        |
|   Miranda)        |  delivered |              |       | rrangement |
|                   |            |              |  days |            |
+-------------------+------------+--------------+-------+------------+
|   Pre-paid        |   50,0     |   Week       |   Pr  |   To be    |
|   offtake         | 00–100,000 | ly/bi-weekly | epaid |            |
|   agreement       |   BBL      |              |       | negotiated |
+-------------------+------------+--------------+-------+------------+
|   Term contract   |   500,000  |   Every 50   |   30  |   To be    |
|   (trading house) |   BBL      |   days       |       |            |
|                   |            |              |  days | negotiated |
+-------------------+------------+--------------+-------+------------+

  If the commercial team secures barge-based sales of 100,000 BBL
  parcels (consistent with the Palmarejo terminal strategy described in
  Section 16), the first revenue event occurs approximately 10 days
  after first oil — enabling true self-funding by Month 12. If sales are
  limited to larger parcels (500,000+ BBL), the self-funding date shifts
  to Month 14-16 as the project must accumulate sufficient inventory
  before the first cargo sale.

  Responsibility delineation: Operations delivers the barrels. The
  External Affairs Director (Roberto Alfonzo) and commercial team must
  secure the sales contracts that convert barrels into cash at the
  frequency required for self-funding. This is a pre-D0 deliverable that
  must be negotiated during the D- phase in parallel with MSA tendering.

Key Insight: The D- Phase Advantage

  By executing 2-3 months of critical preparatory work during D- (while
  PDVSA contract is being finalized), the project achieves first oil
  within 4 weeks of D0 — a timeline that would be impossible under the
  traditional sequential approach where no activity begins until the
  contract is signed. The D- phase effectively “pre-loads” the project
  with 2-3 months of work that would otherwise delay first oil until
  Month 5-6 post-contract.

  This is a fundamental competitive advantage over any operator that
  waits for the PDVSA contract before beginning team mobilization, site
  surveys, and tendering.

  Cost Estimation Methodology: All costs presented in this FDP are
  denominated in United States Dollars (USD) to mitigate local currency
  inflation risks in Venezuela. Sourcing is conducted under a hybrid
  framework: high-technology equipment (ESPs, MPPs, MPFMs, gensets) is
  sourced from international Tier-1 vendors (SLB, Baker Hughes,
  Caterpillar, Emerson) to guarantee reliability, while civil works,
  vehicle fleets, office setups, and logistics are sourced from
  pre-qualified local contractors in Zulia and Caracas to optimize costs
  and support local content [1] [2].

Monthly Project Execution Summary

  Workover-Driven Package Installation Strategy (12 Packages for La Paz)
  Synchronization Logic and Hierarchy (Well → Cluster → Package):

1.  Downhole Equipment Ready (D-2 to D0): All artificial lift equipment
    (ESP pumps, PCPs, GL mandrels, SRP units, cables, VSDs, Xmas Trees)
    are ordered in D-2 and arrive at the wellsite by D0. The workover
    (WO) cannot begin until these are on-site.

2.  Workover Rig (Rig 1): Operates continuously reactivating La Paz
    wells.

    -   Average reactivation time: ESP (8 days), PCP (6 days), GL (7
          days), Rod Pump (5 days).

    -   The rig installs the downhole completion, starts the pump,
          confirms surface flow, and is released after 12 hours to move
          to the next well. The 72-hour test is conducted independently
          by the testing crew without the rig.

Synchronization with Packages:

-   ESP Dedicated (1 Well per Package): Since each ESP requires high
      power and a dedicated VSD, each ESP has its own exclusive package.
      Once the ESP well WO is complete, the corresponding ESP package
      (already available in-country) is installed and commissioned
      within 3 days.

-   PCP, GL, Rod Pump (Clusters): Grouped in clusters of 4 to 6 wells.
      The corresponding package is installed and commissioned once the
      WO of all wells in that cluster is complete.

Package Fabrication in Batches of 4 (Parallel):

-   Batch 1 (D-1 to D+1): 4 packages for La Paz (ESP-1, ESP-2, PCP-1,
      GL-1) fabricated in parallel ($8.38M). Arrive in-country at D+1,
      ready to await workover completion.

-   Batch 2 (D+2 to D+4): 4 packages for La Paz (ESP-3, ESP-4, PCP-2,
      GL-2) fabricated in parallel ($8.38M). Arrive in-country at D+4.

-   Batch 3 (D+6 to D+8): Next 4 packages for La Paz (ESP-5, ESP-6,
      RP-1, RP-2) fabricated in parallel ($8.85M). Arrive in-country at
      D+8.

Package Installation (Synchronized with Workover):

-   D+1 (15 days): ESP well P-207 WO complete → Package ESP-1 installed
      → First Oil ESP-1 (1,881 BOPD)

-   D+2 (30 days): ESP well P-195 WO complete → Package ESP-2 installed
      → First Oil ESP-2 (4,007 BOPD cumulative)

-   D+4 (60 days): 4 PCP wells WO complete → Package PCP-1 installed →
      First Oil PCP-1 (4,265 BOPD cumulative)

-   D+8 (120 days): All 12 La Paz packages installed → La Paz PLATEAU
      (13,191 BOPD)

  This results in an installation rate of 2 packages per month (1 every
  15 days), perfectly synchronized with the workover rig’s capacity to
  reactivate 4 wells every 15 days.

INSTALLATION STRUCTURE AND LOGIC (12 PACKAGES):

1.  Downhole Equipment at D-2: All downhole completion kits for the 32
    La Paz wells (ESP pumps, PCPs, GL mandrels, SRP units, Xmas Trees,
    cables, and VSDs) are ordered at D-2 at a cost of $10.035M. This
    ensures all equipment is already at the wellsite before the workover
    begins, eliminating any logistical delays.

2.  Manifold Installation at D-1: The arrival manifold at FSB is
    installed during the transition week (D-1) at a cost of $0.5M, being
    the first critical EPCM project. Without it, wells cannot be
    connected.

3.  Workover Campaign (WO) as Driver: The workover rig (Rig 1) operates
    continuously, reactivating wells according to the complexity of each
    method (ESP: 8 days, PCP: 6 days, GL: 7 days, Rod Pump: 5 days). The
    rig is released 12 hours after pump start and surface flow
    confirmation, moving immediately to the next well. The 72-hour test
    is conducted independently by the production team.

Installation Synchronization (2 packages per month):

-   ESP Dedicated (1 well per package): Since each ESP requires high
      power and a dedicated VSD, each ESP has its own exclusive package
      ($1.916M). Once the ESP well WO is complete, the corresponding ESP
      package (already available in-country) is installed and
      commissioned within 3 days.

-   PCP, GL, Rod Pump (Clusters): Grouped in clusters of 4 to 6 wells.
      The corresponding package is installed and commissioned once the
      WO of all wells in that cluster is complete.

-   This results in an average of 2 packages installed per month (1
      every 15 days), synchronized with the workover progress.

Package Delivery in Batches of 4 (Synchronized):

-   Batch 1 (D+1): Delivery of the first 4 La Paz packages (ESP-1,
      ESP-2, PCP-1, GL-1) ($4.18M). Arrive at the field and await WO
      completion.

-   Batch 2 (D+4): Delivery of the next 4 La Paz packages (ESP-3, ESP-4,
      PCP-2, GL-2) ($4.18M).

-   Batch 3 (D+7): Delivery of the last 4 La Paz packages (ESP-5, ESP-6,
      RP-1, RP-2) ($4.22M).

-   Redeployment & Refurbishment (D+9, D+11, D+13, D+17): Mara, Mara
      Oeste, La Paz Sur, and El Mojan do not purchase new packages. They
      utilize packages redeployed from La Paz as La Paz wells are
      connected to permanent flowlines. The cost is $2.0M per batch of 4
      for dismantling, transport, refurbishment, and reinstallation only
      (saving over

  $24M in fabrication).

First Oil and Incremental Production:

-   Production from each cluster enters the table immediately in the
      month the package is installed.

-   D+1: ESP-1 online → 1,881 BOPD.

-   D+2: ESP-2 online → 4,007 BOPD.

-   D+8: La Paz fully online → 11,787 BOPD plateau.

-   D+12: La Paz complete with Rod Pumps → 13,191 BOPD plateau.

-   D+24: All 4 fields fully online → 38,000 BOPD plateau.

4.  Gas Export at D+30: The gas pipeline and 800 HP compressor are
    commissioned, initiating gas monetization at D+30 (18 weeks earlier
    than the previous sequential strategy).

MASTER FEED CLASS 3 PACKAGE
Volume A – Engineering Design Basis Memorandum (Working Master)

Document No.: PU-DBM-001
Revision: Rev A (Living Working Draft)
Status: Internal Engineering Use Only
This master document consolidates the engineering philosophy developed
to date and will be expanded under revision control into the complete
FEED Class 3 package.

Document Roadmap

This master document is the governing engineering basis for the
PetroUrdaneta Modular EPF. After completion and approval it will be
frozen as Rev A. The EPC Statement of Work, FEED drawing package and
technical appendices will be derived directly from this document.

1 Executive Summary

PetroUrdaneta will use standardized modular Electric Powered Facilities
(EPFs) to gather untreated multiphase production and transport it to a
Central Processing Facility (CPF). The EPF is a hydraulic transport
station—not a processing plant. Separation, stabilization, gas handling,
produced-water treatment and fiscal metering occur only at the CPF.

2 Development Philosophy

Centralized processing, decentralized gathering, modular skid/trailer
construction, remote operation, minimum permanent infrastructure, and
repeatable field-wide standardization.

3 Process Summary

Producing wells → fail-closed surface safety valves → gathering
flowlines → production manifold → chemical injection → twin-screw
multiphase pump → check valve → pig launcher → export ESD valve →
piggable export pipeline → CPF.

4 Operating Philosophy

Continuous remote operation with a normal crew of four or fewer.
Automatic startup/shutdown where practical. Manual intervention
primarily for maintenance, pigging and periodic well testing.

5 Design Philosophy

No permanent separator, no scrubber, no permanent flare, no permanent
closed-drain system, no permanent multiphase flow meter, no permanent
well test package. Mobile maintenance and centralized processing are
fundamental design principles.

Major Equipment – Purpose and Design Intent

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Tag               Equipment         Purpose           Design Intent
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- --------------------
  MAN-101           Production        Gather wells and  No separation or
                    Manifold          isolate           storage
                                      individually      

  TH-101            Test Header       Temporary mobile  No permanent test
                                      testing           equipment

                                      and control       
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------

Safety and ESD Philosophy

The preferred safe state is achieved by isolation rather than routine
automatic depressurization. On ESD: close wellhead surface safety valves
(through interface), trip multiphase pump, close inlet and export ESD
valves, isolate fuel gas, maintain SIS, SCADA and communications on UPS.
Automatic blowdown is excluded from the base design unless justified by
project hazard studies.

Drain and Vent Philosophy

No permanent hydrocarbon closed-drain vessel or drain header is
provided. Each skid includes capped low-point drains for temporary
connection to a portable sealed recovery tank or vacuum truck. No
permanent flare is included. Maintenance depressurization uses portable
recovery equipment under approved procedures.

Utility Philosophy

Utilities comprise fuel gas from the FSB gas-lift network, gas engine
generator, MCC, UPS, communications, SCADA, LED lighting, earthing and
lightning protection. Instrument air is excluded unless justified by
detailed engineering.

Execution Plan

This document will be expanded chapter-by-chapter to approximately 200
pages. Once frozen, the EPC Statement of Work, FEED drawings and
appendices will be generated directly from it.

9. SAFETY PHILOSOPHY

The primary safety objective of the EPF is to maintain hydrocarbon
containment while protecting personnel, the environment and equipment.
The preferred safe state is isolation of hydrocarbon sources rather than
routine automatic depressurization.

9.1 Safety Objectives

Prevent loss of containment.

Provide rapid isolation of production sources.

Minimize ignition probability.

Provide continuous fire and gas detection.

Permit safe remote operation.

Facilitate safe maintenance using portable recovery equipment.

10. ESD PHILOSOPHY

Emergency Shutdown (ESD) is intended to stop hydrocarbon flow and
isolate the facility. Automatic blowdown is excluded from the base
design unless later hazard studies justify it.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Initiating Event        Automatic Action        Final Safe State
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Manual ESD              Trip pump, close well   Facility isolated
                          SDVs (interface), close 
                          inlet/export ESDVs      

  Confirmed gas detection Trip pump and isolate   Facility isolated
                          hydrocarbons            

  Confirmed fire          Trip pump, isolate      Facility isolated
                          hydrocarbons, isolate   
                          fuel gas                

  Pump high-high          Trip pump, alarm        Production isolated if
  discharge pressure                              required

  Loss of control power   Transfer to UPS,        Safety systems
                          execute fail-safe       maintained
                          actions                 
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

11. WELL INTERFACE PHILOSOPHY

Each well remains outside the EPF battery limits. Wells are equipped
with fail-closed surface safety valves controlled by the field SIS
interface. The base case excludes downhole safety valves; their
application shall be determined through well-specific risk assessment
and regulatory review.

12. METERING PHILOSOPHY

No permanent production metering is provided at the EPF. Field
allocation is based on CPF measurements supported by periodic mobile
well testing.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Permanent                           Temporary
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Pressure, temperature, equipment    Mobile test separator or mobile
  condition monitoring                MPFM connected to test header

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

13. CONTRACTOR DESIGN PRINCIPLES

Keep the facility modular and transportable.

Avoid unnecessary permanent utilities.

Minimize field fabrication and civil works.

Standardize equipment across all EPFs.

Design for remote operation and low maintenance.

Provide future expansion tie-ins.

14. DESIGN INTENT

This chapter defines the Owner's intent behind each major system so that
detailed engineering and EPC proposals remain aligned with the
PetroUrdaneta philosophy.

Production Manifold

Gather and isolate production only; no processing, storage or surge
handling.

Multiphase Pump

Provide hydraulic transport to the CPF; not intended to regulate
production or separate phases.

Pig Launcher

Maintain export line integrity through pigging; not intended for
storage.

Fuel Gas Package

Condition FSB gas only to engine requirements; no gas processing.

Operations Trailer

Provide local supervision and maintenance workspace; not a permanently
staffed control building.

15. OPERABILITY PHILOSOPHY

The EPF shall be capable of unattended operation with periodic operator
visits. Normal staffing shall not exceed four persons. Human
intervention shall be limited to inspections, pigging, chemical
replenishment, planned maintenance and mobile well testing.

Abnormal Operating Scenarios

Loss of generator: transfer critical loads to UPS; controlled shutdown.

Pump trip: isolate production as required; alarm to control room.

Communications loss: continue autonomous safe operation; retain local
control.

Fire or confirmed gas: execute ESD sequence and notify control room.

16. MAINTENANCE PHILOSOPHY

Maintenance shall emphasize modular replacement and portable support
equipment.

No permanent hydrocarbon drain vessel.

No permanent flare.

Portable sealed recovery tank or vacuum truck for maintenance draining.

Portable flare only when required by approved work pack.

Skids designed for replacement rather than extensive field overhaul.

17. INTERFACE SPECIFICATIONS

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Interface               Owner                   Battery Limit
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Well Services           Well Services           Outlet flange of
                          Contractor              gathering flowline

  Artificial Lift         Artificial Lift         Fuel gas take-off and
                          Contractor              well control interface

  EPF                     EPF Contractor          Production manifold to
                                                  export ESD valve

  Pipeline                Pipeline Contractor     Downstream export
                                                  flange

  CPF                     CPF Contractor          Receiving battery limit
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

18. FUTURE EXPANSION PHILOSOPHY

Each EPF shall reserve physical space, electrical capacity and process
tie-ins for a second pump, additional wells, future metering and
additional chemical injection without major reconstruction.

20. DESIGN ENVELOPE

The Contractor shall validate equipment performance over the complete
production life of the field rather than at a single operating point.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Parameter               Basis                   Contractor Action
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Production rate         Minimum, normal and     Confirm hydraulic
                          maximum                 suitability

  Gas volume fraction     Full anticipated        Verify pump capability
                          operating envelope      

  Water cut               Field life profile      Demonstrate operability

  Slug size/frequency     Hydraulic study         Confirm transient
                                                  performance

  Sand production         Reservoir prediction    Select suitable
                                                  materials

  Future expansion        Additional wells and    Reserve plot space and
                          second pump             utilities
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Purpose and Design Intent

23. HYDRAULIC DESIGN PHILOSOPHY

The EPF shall be designed as a hydraulic transportation facility.
Hydraulic performance shall be validated over the anticipated field
life, including minimum, normal and maximum production rates.

Transient hydraulic analysis shall verify start-up, shutdown and ESD
conditions.

Slug flow shall be accommodated by pump selection and operating envelope
rather than upstream separation.

Pressure losses shall be minimized through manifold layout and piping
design.

The export system shall remain fully piggable.

Thermal relief shall be provided for blocked-in liquid sections.

24. FLOW ASSURANCE PHILOSOPHY

Flow assurance shall rely on proactive design rather than permanent
process equipment.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Threat                  Primary Mitigation      Secondary Mitigation
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Wax                     Chemical injection      Routine pigging

  Scale                   Scale inhibitor         Water chemistry
                                                  monitoring

  Corrosion               Corrosion inhibitor     Inspection program

  Sand                    Material selection      Monitoring and
                                                  maintenance

  Hydrates (if            Operating envelope      Temporary chemical
  applicable)                                     treatment
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

25. RELIABILITY, AVAILABILITY AND MAINTAINABILITY (RAM)

The EPF shall emphasize simplicity, standardization and rapid
restoration of service.

Standardized equipment across all EPFs.

Skid replacement preferred over extensive field repair.

Critical spares standardized.

Remote diagnostics integrated into SCADA.

Preventive maintenance based on OEM recommendations and condition
monitoring.

26. INSTRUMENTATION PHILOSOPHY

Instrumentation shall be minimized while maintaining safe operation and
equipment protection.

Pressure and temperature monitoring at key process locations.

Independent shutdown sensors where credited by the SIS.

Vibration and bearing temperature monitoring for rotating equipment.

Valve position indication on shutdown valves.

Remote health monitoring through SCADA.

27. ELECTRICAL DESIGN CRITERIA

The electrical system shall provide autonomous operation using fuel gas
supplied from the FSB gas-lift network.

Gas engine generator sized for peak continuous load with defined design
margin.

UPS shall support SIS, PLC, RTU, communications and emergency lighting.

Automatic load restoration following generator restart.

Earthing and lightning protection for all skids and trailers.

28. MECHANICAL DESIGN CRITERIA

Mechanical design shall prioritize transportability, maintainability and
standardized fabrication.

Road-transportable skid dimensions where practicable.

Bolted modular construction preferred over field welding.

Lifting points certified for repeated relocation.

Materials selected for produced fluids and anticipated corrosion
environment.

Pressure-containing equipment designed to applicable project codes.

29. PRELIMINARY OPERATIONS MANUAL (CONCEPT)

Operating procedures will be developed during FEED and finalized during
EPC. The concept manual shall include:

Pre-startup inspection

Normal startup

Normal shutdown

Emergency shutdown

Pigging operations

Mobile well testing

Maintenance isolation and portable recovery

Restart after trip

Communications failure

Generator failure response

30. DESIGN BASIS AND PROJECT ASSUMPTIONS

This chapter establishes the common engineering assumptions to be used
by all disciplines. Unless otherwise approved by PetroUrdaneta, all
engineering, calculations, drawings and vendor packages shall conform to
these assumptions.

Centralized processing at CPF.

Untreated multiphase transport from wells to CPF.

Normal operating crew not exceeding four personnel.

Modular skid/trailer construction.

Fuel gas supplied from FSB gas-lift system.

No permanent separator, flare, MPFM or hydrocarbon closed-drain system.

Portable maintenance recovery philosophy.

31. PRELIMINARY FUNCTIONAL DESIGN SPECIFICATION (FDS) – MULTIPHASE PUMP
PACKAGE

Purpose: Provide hydraulic energy to transport untreated multiphase
production to the CPF.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Function                            Requirement
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Control                             Automatic VFD speed control with
                                      local/manual capability

  Protection                          High vibration, high bearing
                                      temperature, motor protection, high
                                      discharge pressure

  Monitoring                          Pressure, temperature, vibration,
                                      speed, motor current

  Shutdown                            Trip on SIS command or package
                                      protection

  Maintainability                     Skid-mounted with lifting
                                      provisions and removable components
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

33. CONTROL NARRATIVE (SUMMARY)

The control philosophy shall distinguish between process control,
equipment protection and safety instrumented functions.

Normal production pressure controlled by pump VFD.

Chemical injection proportional to production strategy.

ESD logic implemented independently from basic process control where
safety functions are credited.

Remote operation through SCADA with local override for maintenance.

Restart permitted only after trip cause has been cleared and operator
authorization obtained.

Formal PFD (PU-PFD-001)

Formal P&IDs (PU-PID-001 to 004)

35. PROCESS DESIGN CRITERIA

This chapter establishes mandatory process design criteria for all FEED
and EPC activities. The Contractor shall validate the final design
against the approved production forecast and hydraulic model.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Criterion                           Requirement
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Production Fluid                    Untreated oil, produced water,
                                      associated gas and anticipated
                                      solids.

  Operating Philosophy                Continuous multiphase transport
                                      with no intentional phase
                                      separation.

  Turndown                            Continuous operation over the
                                      expected field production decline.

  Hydraulic Basis                     Steady-state and transient analysis
                                      required.

  Flow Assurance                      Chemical inhibition and pigging are
                                      the primary mitigation methods.

  Future Expansion                    Reserve capacity for additional
                                      wells and a second pump.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

37. HAZOP DESIGN INTENT

The following design intents shall be preserved during HAZOP unless
PetroUrdaneta approves a change:

No permanent separators.

No permanent flare.

No permanent hydrocarbon closed-drain system.

No routine depressurization following ESD.

Fail-safe isolation is preferred over inventory blowdown.

Portable maintenance recovery equipment is the standard maintenance
philosophy.

38. OWNER ACCEPTANCE CRITERIA

The EPF design will be accepted only when the Contractor demonstrates:

Compliance with the Design Basis Memorandum.

Successful hydraulic and transient studies.

Documented operating envelope for the multiphase pump.

Complete Cause & Effect and SIS verification.

Modular transportability by road.

Maintainability with a four-person operating philosophy.

Provision for future expansion without major reconstruction.

Before issue for EPC tender, the following minimum maturity is expected:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

40. Process Control Philosophy

The BPCS regulates production transport while the SIS independently
performs credited safety functions. Pump discharge pressure is
controlled through VFD speed control. Local control is limited to
commissioning and maintenance; normal operation is remote through SCADA.

41. Alarm Management Philosophy

Alarms shall be prioritized as High, Medium and Low. Only actionable
alarms shall be presented to operators. High-priority alarms include
fire, confirmed gas and ESD events.

42. Cybersecurity & Communications

Operational technology networks shall be segregated from enterprise
networks. Remote access shall be authenticated, logged and controlled.

43. Spares Philosophy

Critical equipment shall be standardized across all EPFs. Long-lead
spares shall be identified during FEED and stocked centrally.

44. Inspection, Testing & Commissioning Basis

Packaged equipment shall undergo FAT, integrated control testing, SAT,
commissioning and performance verification before acceptance.

45. Document Register

Controlled documents shall include DBM, EPC SOW, PFDs, P&IDs, GA
drawings, SLDs, Cause & Effect matrices, Functional Design
Specifications and Equipment Datasheets.

46. Mechanical Design Philosophy

The mechanical design shall prioritize simplicity, transportability,
maintainability and repeatability.
Every process package shall be skid mounted and designed for
transportation by conventional road trailers.
Field welding shall be minimized. Major equipment shall be replaceable
without dismantling adjacent skids.
Rotating equipment shall be accessible for maintenance using a mobile
crane without removing permanent structures.

Mechanical Requirements

Design for repeated transport loads.

Provide certified lifting points and transport restraints.

Bolted construction preferred over field welding.

Thermal relief valves on blocked-in liquid sections.

Double block and bleed where maintenance isolation is required.

All drain points fitted with blind flange or capped connection.

47. Piping Philosophy

The piping system is intended only to transport untreated multiphase
production. The piping layout shall minimise pressure loss, eliminate
unnecessary fittings and remain completely piggable from the EPF to the
CPF.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  System                  Objective               Requirement
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Gathering               Transport wells to      Full-bore isolation,
                          manifold                future tie-ins

  Pump suction            Uniform inlet           Minimum fittings
                          conditions              

  Pump discharge          Stable transport        Check valve, thermal
                                                  relief

  Export line             Reliable transport      100% piggable
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

48. Valve Philosophy

Valves shall be selected to maximize reliability and minimize
maintenance.

Full-port ball valves for isolation where pigging is required.

Fail-closed actuated ESD valves at EPF inlet and export.

Check valve immediately downstream of pump.

Dedicated maintenance vent and drain valves.

Thermal relief valves on blocked liquid sections.

49. Instrument Philosophy

Instrumentation shall support operation, equipment protection and
functional safety while minimizing lifecycle maintenance.

Pressure transmitters at manifold, pump suction and discharge.

Temperature monitoring on suction/discharge and bearings.

Independent shutdown transmitters where required by SIS.

Vibration monitoring on rotating equipment.

Valve position feedback for all ESD valves.

50. Utility Architecture

Only essential utilities shall be permanently installed.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Utility                             Design Basis
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Fuel Gas                            Taken from FSB gas-lift network
                                      through filter/coalescer and
                                      regulator

  Electrical                          Gas engine generator plus UPS

  Communications                      Fiber, radio or satellite with
                                      redundant path where justified

  Lighting                            LED perimeter and maintenance
                                      lighting

  Instrument Air                      Not included unless justified
                                      during detailed engineering
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

52. FEED Action Register

Before freezing Revision A the following shall be completed:
• Complete hydraulic model.
• Complete HAZID and HAZOP.
• Confirm well shutdown philosophy.
• Confirm generator sizing.
• Complete PFDs and P&IDs.
• Issue equipment datasheets.
• Finalize Cause & Effect.
• Freeze battery limits and interfaces.
• Approve Design Basis Memorandum.
• Generate EPC Statement of Work directly from the approved DBM.
